WORD: aggregate
DEFN: 1. A mixture of minerals or rock fragments that resembles rock or any material, which can be cemented together to form a solid material. They are used in road building, laying foundation of buildings and in producing building materials as bricks, blocks and cement. 2. Aggregates are soil units containing mostly clay, silt and sand particles held together by a gel-type substance formed by organic matter. There are three main types of aggregates: (i) platelike , in which the aggregates are flat and mostly horizontal; (ii) prismlike , which forms vertical than horizontal dimension; and (iii) blocklike , which is roughly equal in horizontal and vertical dimensions and either angular or rounded. 3. In botany, aggregates are random associations of animals with little or no internal organisation. They form in response to a single stimulus and disperse when the stimulus is removed. It is one of the three broad classes of social organisation. 4. In computer programming languages, a structured collection of data objects that form a data type.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aggression
DEFN: Behaviour used to frighten or injure another organism usually for purposes of gaining a territory, a mate or food. It often involves series of threats without having to engage in potentially dangerous physical contact. Examples include roaring by red deer, snarling by dogs, fluffing up of feathers by birds or raising of fins by some species of fish.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: agree
DEFN: When two functions have the same values for the same argument, we say that the functions agree on the set S if f ( x ) = g ( x ) for all x in S. The sets of measurable functions that agree almost everywhere form equivalence classes under this relation.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: air bag
DEFN: 1. A safety device fitted in motor vehicles consisting of an inflatable plastic bag mounted under the dashboard or on the back of the front seat and also sometimes in the roof and doors. It inflates automatically in an accident and prevents the passengers from being thrown forwards. A person in the moving car has a momentum. This momentum must be brought to zero when the car suddenly stops as a result of collision. Since the person's momentum must be reduced to zero in a very short time, it implies that the force on the person's body must be very high (Newton's second law of Motion: F = Δ mv /Δ t ). The air bag extends the interval of time over which the person's momentum is reduced to zero and as a result reduces the rate of change of momentum and hence, the force. 2. Any of a number of large inflatable bags comprising part of a spacecraft's landing system, enabling the craft to come to rest through a series of bounces to provide a softer, protective surface for a person to strike on in case of collision.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: air mass
DEFN: A qualitative term to describe a widespread body of air with approximately uniform characteristics which had been identified at a given time over a particular region of the earth's surface. Sometimes an air mass is marked by inert tracers such as SF6 which may be added to it. The composition of a given air mass undergoes alteration as it migrates, chemical changes occur, compounds are removed by dry and wet deposition and new impurities are added to the mass. An air mass forms when air rests over an area long enough to pick up the conditions of that area. It has distinct boundaries and may extend hundreds or thousands of miles horizontally and sometimes as high as the top of the troposphere. In astronomy, air mass is the optical path length through the Earth's atmosphere for light from a celestial source. As it passes through the atmosphere, light is attenuated by scattering and absorption. The more atmosphere through which it passes, the greater the attenuation. Consequently, celestial bodies at the horizon appear less bright than when at the zenith. The attenuation, known as atmospheric extinction, is described quantitatively by the Beer-Lambert-Bouguer law. Air mass normally indicates relative air mass , the path length relative to that at the zenith at sea level, so by definition, the sea-level air mass at the zenith is 1. Air mass increases as the angle between the source and the zenith increases, reaching a value of approximately 38 at the horizon. Air mass can be less than one at an elevation greater than sea level; however, most closed-form expressions for air mass do not include the effects of elevation, so adjustment must usually be accomplished by other means. In some fields, such as solar energy and photovoltaics, A air mass is indicated by the acronym AM. The value of the air mass is often given by appending its value to AM, so that AM1 indicates an air mass of 1, AM2 indicates an air mass of 2, etc. The region above the Earth's atmosphere, where there is no atmospheric attenuation of solar radiation, is considered to have "air mass zero" (AM0).
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: air pollutant
DEFN: A gaseous substance or aerosol which has been introduced into the air either by human activity or by natural processes in sufficient concentration to produce a measurable effect on humans, animals, vegetation or materials. Examples of air pollutants are sulfur dioxide (SO2 ), nitrogen dioxide (NO2 ), particulate matter (PM10 ) or suspended particulates, carbon monoxide (CO) and ozone (O3 ). Air pollution also leads to effects such as increased UV radiation due to the depletion of the ozone layer, acid rain, greenhouse effect and photochemical smog.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: air pollution index
DEFN: A mathematical combination of the concentrations of air pollutants which gives an approximate numerical measure of the quality of the air at a given time. It is a non-linear index that runs from 0 to 500 for reporting daily air quality to the public. The higher the value, the greater the level of air pollution and the greater the health concern. For example, a value of 50 represents good air quality with little potential to affect public health, while a value over 300 represents hazardous air quality. The API level is based on the level of five atmospheric pollutants, which are sulfur dioxide (SO2 ), nitrogen dioxide (NO2 ), particulate matter (PM10 ) or suspended particulates, carbon monoxide (CO) and ozone (O3 ) measured at the monitoring stations throughout each city.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: airy function
DEFN: Any of the functions Ai( z ) and Bi( z ), which are the special solutions of the second-order differential equation ∅ '' = t ∅ = 0; which is ∅ ( t ) = 1 π cos tx + x 3 ⎛ ⎝ ⎜⎜⎜⎜ ⎞ ⎠ ⎟⎟⎟⎟⎟ ∞ ∫ dx . There are four varieties of Airy functions: Ai( z ), Bi( z ), Gi( z ) and Hi( z ). Ai( z ) and Bi( z ) are used more frequently than Gi( z ) and Hi( z ). Airy functions commonly appear in physics, especially in optics, quantum mechanics, electromagnetics and radiative transfer.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aitken particles
DEFN: Aerosol particles, which are less than 0.1 µm in diameter and are small enough to get into the human respiratory system. These particles are the most abundant in the atmosphere when classifying atmospheric particles by size. Their concentrations can be determined with the Aitken counter which measures total particle number density. Owing to their small size, Aitken particles contribute little to the total mass concentration of all aerosol particles; this is determined primarily by particles less than 0.1 µm in diameter.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aitken's method
DEFN: A numerical method used to accelerate the convergence of an equation. Normally, the formula X t +1 = f ( X t ) is used to solve the equation. The method uses initial value and the first two values obtained by the formula, to calculate a good approximation than the results of the iterative formula.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alabaster
DEFN: A naturally-occurring, fine-grained, white or light coloured, translucent form of gypsum with chemical formula, CaSO4 .2H2 O. It is used for decorative carving and the production of dry lining boards for the building industry. It is also used as a soil conditioner for heavy, poorly draining, soils where the included sulfur also aids plant growth by reducing the alkalinity. A small quantity is also included in Portland cement to control the set time.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alanine
DEFN: Any of the two non- essential amino acids with the chemical formula C3 H7 NO2 , molecular mass 89.09 gmol^-1 , density 1.424 g/cm3 and melting point 258°C (531 K). Alpha-alanine is one of the nonessential amino acids, found in most proteins and particularly abundant in fibroin, the protein in silk. It is used in research and as a dietary supplement. Beta-alanine is a naturally occurring amino acid not found in proteins. It is an important constituent of the vitamin pantothenic acid and is used in its synthesis, as well as in biochemical research, electroplating and organic synthesis.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alaoglu theorem
DEFN: The theorem that in a dual Banach space the unit ball is weak star compact and, more generally, that the polar of a neighbourhood of the origin in a topological vector space is also weak star compact.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: albite
DEFN: A mineral, with the chemical formula NaAlSi 3 O8 , Mohs scale hardness of 6 - 6.5 and specific gravity of 2.60 - 2.65 that occurs in igneous rocks. It often forms vitreous crystals of various colours. It is a variety of plagioclase, one of the three end-members (pure compounds) of the feldspar group.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alcian blue
DEFN: A copper phthalocyanin stain, with the chemical formula C56 H58 Cl 14 CuN16 S4 that enhances the preservation of cell surface materials. It is usually used during fixation with glutaraldehyde for transmission electron microscopy and also used as a stain for connective tissue mucins and a number of epithelial mucins and as a gelling agent for lubricating fluids.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alcohol
DEFN: 1. Common name for ethanol, ethyl alcohol or grain alcohol. Its chemical formula is C2 H5 OH. 2. Any organic compound whose molecules have one or more hydroxyl groups attached to saturated carbon atoms. The general formula is C n H2n+1 OH. Examples include methanol, propanol and glycerol. They react with organic acids to form esters. They are used in industries as solvents and for medical substances in pharmacy.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aldaric acids
DEFN: Polyhydroxy dicarboxylic acids having the general formula HOC(=O)[CH(OH)] n C(=O)OH. They are formed by oxidation of both terminal groups of an aldose to carboxyl groups.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aldehydes
DEFN: Organic compounds with the general formula RC(=O)H, in which a carbon atom is bonded to one hydrogen atom with a single covalent bond and oxygen atom with a double covalent bond and single-bonded to another chemical group such as methane, benzene, another hydrogen, etc. The carbon oxygen double bond part is known as a carbonyl group (C=O), often referred to as a formyl or methanoyl group. Aldehydes are formed by oxidation of primary alcohols. Further oxidation yields carboxylic acids. Examples of aldehydes are methanol (formaldehyde) and ethanol (acetaldehyde). Ethanol is a carbonyl group single-bonded to a hydrogen and single-bonded to a methane. They are reducing agents. Some other types of organic compounds containg carbonyl groups are ketones and carboxylic acids.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aldicarb
DEFN: A crystalline, white solid having slightly sulfurous smell with the chemical formula C7 H14 N2 N2 S, molar mass 190.26 gmol^-1 , density 1.195 g/cm³, melting point 100 °C (373 K) and decomposes before boiling point. It is barely soluble in water but readily dissolves in organic solvents and it is used as systemic insecticide, acaricide and nematicide.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aldimine
DEFN: Any imine derived from aldehydes. The general structure is RCH = NR, where R may be any organyl group or H.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aldonic acids
DEFN: Polyhydroxy acids such as D-gluconic acid and D-gluconolactone having the general formula HOCH2 [CH(OH)] n C(=O)OH. They are derived from an aldose by oxidation of the aldehydic group.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aldose
DEFN: Type of monosaccharide or simple sugar such as glucose and fructose with the chemical formula C n (H2 O) n and whose molecule A contains an aldehyde (-CHO) group and one or more alcohol (-OH) groups.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aldoximes
DEFN: Compounds formed by reaction between hydroxylamine and aldehyde RCHO + H2 NOH → RCH:NOH +H2 O. If R is an aliphatic group, the aldoxime is generally a liquid or low melting solid. If R is aromatic, the aldoxime is a crystalline solid. Aldoximes are useful in qualitative analysis of aldehydes.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aldrin
DEFN: A white crystalline water-insoluble organo-chlorine pesticide with the chemical formula C12 H8 Cl 6 . It is soluble in solvents such as acetone, benzene, ethanol and xylene and stable in strong alkali. It is used as soil insecticide mainly against termites. Aldrin is a persistent organic pollutant, which has been banned.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aleph
DEFN: In set theory, they are a sequence of infinite numbers used to represent the cardinality or size of infinite sets. They are represented by the symbol ℵ , the first Hebrew letter. Aleph-null , also known as aleph-nought is the smallest infinite cardinal number and is a set of cardinal numbers that can be put into a bijection or one-to-one correspondence between it and the natural numbers, which implies that it is countably infinite; the sets are countable or denumerable. It is represented by ℵ 0 . .Aleph-one, with the symbol ℵ 1 , is the cardinality of the set of all countable ordinal numbers, called ω 1 or Ω. It is an ordinal number larger than all countable ones; therefore it is an uncountable set.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alexander's sub-base theorem
DEFN: The theorem put forward by James Waddle Alexander (1888-1971), US mathematician and topologist that if every open cover of a topological space, by elements of a sub-base has a finite sub-cover, then the space is compact. More formally, if S is a sub-base for the topology of a space X and any cover of X by elements of S has a finite sub-cover, then X is compact.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alexandroff one-point compactification
DEFN: A compactification that adds a single point, designated ∞, to a Hausdorffspace and adds complements of closed subsets of the original space as neighbourhoods of this point. The real numbers can be compactified in this manner.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebra
DEFN: 1. A system of arithmetic that applies to any set of non- numerical symbols, usually letters, axioms and rules by which they are combined or operated upon. In other words, it is the branch of mathematics in which symbols, usually letters of alphabets, represent numbers or quantities and express general relationship that hold for all members of a specified set, for example x + y = y + x . 2. Also called abstract algebra , it is the study of structures that deal with algebraic systems rather than the usual number systems and includes groups, rings and fields. 3. Any formal calculus used to model and study the properties of the entities that are the intended interpretation of their symbols, such as the algebra of logic and the algebra of classes. 4. A Boolean algebra or sigma-algebra (σ-algebra) and in particular, the algebra of subsets or algebra of propositions. 5. Any formal system with only functions and constants, but no relations except possibly identity.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebra of sets
DEFN: The properties and laws of sets, which includes the set-theoretic operations of union ( ∪ ), intersection ( ∩ ) and complementation and the relations of set equality and set inclusion. It also provides systematic procedures for evaluating expressions and performing calculations, involving these operations and relations. The binary operations of set union ( ∪ ) and intersection ( ∩ ) satisfy many identities. Several of these identities or laws have the following names: (i) Commutative laws: A ∪ B = B ∪ A and A ∩ B = B ∩ A; (ii) Associative laws: A ∪ (B ∪ C) = (A ∪ B) U C and (A ∩ B) ∩ C = A ∩ (B ∩ C). (iii) Distributive laws : A ∪ (B ∩ C) = (A ∪ B) ∩ (A ∪ C) and A ∩ (B ∪ C) = (A ∩ B) ∪ (A ∩ C). There are also some more important laws of set algebra. For example, Proposition 3 states that for any subsets A and B of a universal set ∪ , the following identities hold: idempotent laws given by (i) A ∪ A = A and (ii) A ∩ A = A. The de Morgan's laws are given by (i) (A ∪ B)' = A' ∩ B' and (ii) (A ∩ B)' = A' ∪ B' .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebra over a field
DEFN: A ring that is also a vector space in which the scalars are members of a field that satisfies the condition that if x and y are any members of the ring and a and b are any scalars, then ( a x ) ( b y ) = ( ab )( xy ).
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic
DEFN: 1. Of, relating to or involving algebra or using only finite numbers, expressions and only the operations of addition, subtraction, multiplication, division and exponentiation with constant rational exponents. 2. Consisting of or relating to the roots of a polynomial equation with rational coefficients. Also, algebraic over a field is about the roots of a polynomial equation with coefficients that are members of the given field.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic addition theorem
DEFN: 1. Any theorem or identity that gives an addition formula for a function f in terms of a polynomial P in three complex variables such that P ( f ( x ), f ( y ), f ( x + y )) = 0 for all complex arguments. 2. A classical result in elliptic function theory that for every elliptic function there is an algebraic addition theorem. A meromorphic function has such an addition theorem if and only if it is rational, trigonometric or elliptic. For example, exp( x + y ) = exp( x ) exp( y ) is an addition theorem for the exponential with P ( x , y , z ) = xy = z .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic closure
DEFN: It is the inclusion of all the roots of polynomials with coefficients of a given set, when the given set is extended. An example is x 2 + 1 = 0, which has the smallest algebraic closed set with complex solution.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic curve
DEFN: A plane curve satisfying a polynomial equation in two variables x and y , for example, p ( x , y ) = 0. These are functions that, in their most general form, can be written as a sum of polynomials in x multiplied by powers of y , equals to zero. A curve in the real plane that is algebraic is defined by p ( x , y ) = 0, where p is a real polynomial. For example, if p = x 2 + y 2 - 1, then p ( x , y ) = 0 defines a circle. Examples of algebraic curves are (i) quadratic curve , an algebraic curve of degree two and includes circle, ellipse, hyperbola,and parabola. In standard form, a quadratic function is written as y = ax 2 + bx + c . (ii) Cubic curve , an algebraic curve of degree three. Examples are the cissoid of Diocles, conchoid of de Sluze, folium of Descartes, Maclaurin trisectrix, Maltese cross curve, right strophoid, semicubical parabola and serpentine curve. (iii) Quartic Curve , an algebraic curve of degree four. Examples are ampersand curve, bean curve, bicorn, bicuspid curve, bifoliate, bifolium, bitangent-rich curve, bow, bullet nose, butterfly curve, capricornoid, cardioid and Cartesian ovals. (iv) Quintic Curve , an algebraic curve of degree five. Examples include the Burnside curve, butterfly catastrophe curve and stirrup curve. (v) Sextic Curve , an algebraic curve of degree six. Examples include the astroid, atriphtaloid, Cayley's sextic, cornoid, cycloid of Ceva, dumbbell curve, ellipse evolute, epicycloid, Freeth's nephroid and heart curve (first). (vi) Octic Curve is an algebraic curve of degree eight, for example, the pear curve. (vii) Dodecic curve is an algebraic curve of degree twelve, for example, the ranunculoid. x y n Quadratic curve, an algebraic curve of order two y a<0 a>0 x y = ax + c,where n = , where n = -2, -1, 1, 2 and 3.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic equation
DEFN: An equation of the form f ( x ) = 0, where f is a polynomial. It shows the equality of two expressions formulated by applying to a set of variables, the algebraic operations namely, addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, raising to power and extraction of root. An example is 4 x 3 + 6 x 2 - 7 x + 4 = 0.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic expression
DEFN: An expression containing a collection of numbers, variables and signs, positive or negative and arithmetic operations that must make mathematical and logical sense, for example, 5 x 3 - 3 y 2 + 2 xy . Two types exist: simple and compound. Simple algebraic expression has one term, called a monomial, for example, 3 a . Compound algebraic expression has two or more terms: the one with two terms, for example, 4 x + 3 y is a binomial; the one with three terms, for example, 2 x 2 - 3 x + 1, 3 m^-2 n + 5 z , is a trinomial; and the one with more than three terms is a multinomial. Alternatively, an algebraic expression may not contain any individual quantifiers and is expressed only in terms of variables and intended to be interpreted as being true for all members of some set; for example, x ( y + z ) = xy + xz .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic extension
DEFN: An extension field of a base field with the property that every element of the extension is algebraic over the base. Let L/K be an extension of fields. L/K is said to be an algebraic extension field if every element of L is algebraic over K. If L/K is not algebraic and contains transcendental elements then it is a transcendental extension field. An algebraic extension of the rationals is an algebraic number field. For example, the field extension R/Q, that is the field of real numbers as an extension of the field of rational numbers, is transcendental, while the field extensions C/R and Q(√2)/Q are algebraic, where C is the field of complex numbers.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic function
DEFN: A function that may be expressed in a finite number of terms, of only the elementary algebraic operations of addition, subtraction, multiplication, division and root extraction, for example, f ( x ) = nx 3 + x 4 - 2 x . They are distinguished from transcendental functions, such as the exponential, logarithmic and trigonometric functions, which can be expressed only by an infinite series. Another way of saying this is that the function f ( x ) is algebraic if y = f ( x ) is a solution of an equation of the form p n ( x ) y n + ... + p 1 ( x ) + p 0 ( x ) = 0, where p 0 ( x ), p 1 ( x ), ..., p n ( x ) are polynomials in x . All polynomials are algebraic. A function that satisfies no such equation is transcendental. There are various types of algebraic functions. A rational function does not have any fractional powers of the variable or variables. Integral functions do not include the operation of division in any of their terms. A homogeneous function is one in which the terms are all of the same degree, i.e., the sum of the indices of the variables in each term is the same for every term. For example, u 4 + u 3 v + u 2 v 2 + uv 3 + v 4 is a rational, integral, homogeneous function of the fourth degree in u and v .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic geometry
DEFN: The study of geometries that come from algebra, in particular, from rings. In classical algebraic geometry, the algebra is the ring of polynomials and the geometry is the set of zeros of polynomials, called an algebraic variety . For instance, the unit circle is the set of zeros of x 2 + y 2 = 1 is an algebraic variety, as in all the conic sections.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic independence
DEFN: Let f 1 , ..., f n be polynomials in F[ x 1 ,..., x n ]; { f 1 , ..., f n } are algebraically independent if and only if there is no non-zero polynomial A ∈ F[ y 1 , ..., y n ] such that A( f 1 , ..., f n ) = 0. If the polynomials do not satisfy this, they are said to be algebraically dependent and A is their annihilating polynomial. For example, { x 1 , x 2 } are algebraically independent, but { x 1 , x 2 , x 1 3 + x 2 2 } are not.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic integer
DEFN: The root of a polynomial with integer coefficients, where the coefficient of the highest power is 1. The polynomial is of the form x n + a 1 x n^-1 + ... + a n = 0, where a 1 , ..., a n are integers; For example, x 1 = 2 + 3 is an algebraic integer, since A x 1 2 + 4 x 1 + 1 = 0 . The set of all algebraic integers is closed under addition and multiplication and therefore, a subring of complex numbers denoted by A.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic number
DEFN: A number that is the root of an algebraic polynomial. For example, in the expression x 2 = 2, the square root of 2 (√2) is an algebraic number because it is a solution of the equation x 2 = 2. Another source defines it as any number that is the root of a polynomial equation with coefficients drawn from a given field, in particular the rationals; in that case, √2 is an algebraic number, but π is not. A number which is not algebraic is called transcendental. The algebraic numbers form a field.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic operation
DEFN: Any of the operations of addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, raising to a power or extraction of roots. Operations that involve infinite series and functions such as log x are not algebraic (they depend on the use of limits). The term algebraic is also applied to arithmetic operations carried out with due regard to sign. Thus, the algebraic sum of a and - b is a - b , the algebraic product of - a and - b is ab , etc.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic sum
DEFN: The total of a set of quantities, with regard to their signs, as + or -, according to the rules of addition in algebra. For example, the algebraic sum of^-2, 8 and^-1 is 5.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraic variety
DEFN: The set of solutions of a system of polynomial equations, over the real or complex numbers. In other words, it is a subset of an n -dimensional algebraic geometry, consisting of all points ( x 1 , ..., x n +1 ) satisfying a system of polynomial equations P α x 1 ,...,x n + 1 ( ) = α ∈ A . It is the main ingredients of the study of algebraic geometry.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algebraically soluble
DEFN: Of a first-order ordinary differential equation, such that, where u and t are respectively the dependent and independent variables, either it can be solved for p = du / dt and the resulting first-degree equation in p can be solved; or else it can be solved for u , giving an equation that can be differentiated to give a first-order equation in the dependent variable t and independent variable p ; or else it can be solved for t ; giving an equation that can be differentiated with respect to u to give a linear first-order equation in the independent variable p , after writing dt / dp = 1/ p .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algin
DEFN: 1. A complex polysaccharide occurring in the cell walls of the brown algae. It is produced commercially from species of Laminaria and Macrocystis in the form of alginates which are used as stabilisers and texturing agents in the food industry. 2. A yellowish-white organic solid, with the chemical formula (C6 H8 O6 ) n . It is a polymer of mannuronic acid in the pyranose ring form that occurs in brown seaweeds. It has many industrial applications due to the high level of viscosity of its dilute solutions.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: algorithm
DEFN: 1. Procedure systematic steps for executing an operation procedure or series of steps, for example, by mechanical means, that can be used to effect a particular calculation or to manipulate data in a certain way, usually to solve a particular problem in mathematics and computer science. In elementary arithmetic, simple algorithms are those used in the extraction of square roots and in long division. In computer science, the term is commonly used in the context of programming. The word is derived from the name of the 9th-century mathematician, Ibn-Masa al-Khwarizmi. 2. A recursive definition enabling any member of an infinite sequence of terms to be generated by its repeated application.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aliquant part
DEFN: A number or an expression which is not a precise divisor of a given number or expression. For example, x + 3 is an aliquot of x 2 + 9 and 3 is an aliquant part of 10.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alizarin
DEFN: An orange-red dye, with the chemical formula C14 H8 O4 , which forms lakes when heavy metal salts are added to its alkaline solutions. It occurs naturally in madder ( Rubia tinctorum ), but is generally synthesised by heating anthraquinone with sodium hydroxide.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alkaline phosphatase
DEFN: An enzyme catalysing the hydrolysis of phosphoric esters under conditions of alkaline pH. In humans the blood levels of alkaline phosphatase are measured as part of the assessment of liver function. In the blood the enzyme also breaks down phosphates required for the mineralisation of the bone. There are two distinct forms of the enzyme, the alkaline phosphatase and bone alkaline phosphatase. It is found in bacteria, fungi and animals but not in higher plants.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alkanes
DEFN: A group of saturated open-chain hydrocarbon containing only carbon-to-carbon or carbon-to-hydrogen single bonds and belonging to a series with general formula C n H2n+2 which is commonly known as paraffin. In nature they are found in natural gas and petroleum. They are chemically inert, inflammable and stable.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alkanium ion
DEFN: Any carbocation obtained by hydrogenation of an alkane and containing at least one pentacoordinate carbon atom. Examples are methanium ( + CH5 ) and ethanium [C2 H7 ] + . Primary alkanol H C H OH CHHH Secondary alkanol Tertiary alkanol HCHHCH OH HCHHCCHHCHHH OH HHCHH.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alkenes
DEFN: Acyclic branched or unbranched hydrocarbons whose molecules contain one or more carbon-carbon double bonds having the general formula C n H2n . Examples are ethane and propene. Examples of acyclic branched or unbranched hydrocarbons having more than one double bond are alkadienes and alkatrienes.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alkyl groups
DEFN: Univalent groups derived from alkanes by removal of a hydrogen atom from any carbon atom -C n H2n+1 . The groups A derived by removal of a hydrogen atom from a terminal carbon atom of unbranched alkanes form a subclass of normal alkyl ( n -alkyl) groups H(CH2 ) n . The groups RCH2 , R2 CH (R ≠ H) and R3 C (R ≠ H) are primary, secondary and tertiary alkyl groups, respectively.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alkyl radicals
DEFN: Carbon-centred radicals derived formally by removal of one hydrogen atom from an alkane. An example is propyl (C3 H7 ).
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: allenes
DEFN: Compounds that contain cumulate double bonds, >C=C=C<, in which three carbon atoms are linked by two adjacent double bonds. The outer carbon atoms are each linked to two other atoms or groups by single bonds. The simplest example is 1,2-propadiene, CH2 =C=CH2 . Allenes are dienes (unsaturated hydrocarbon alkene containing two carbon-to-carbon double bonds) with typical reactions of alkenes. Under basic conditions, they often convert to alkynes. In an allene, the two double bonds lie in planes that are perpendicular to each other. Consequently, in an allene of the type R1 R2 C=C=CR3 R4 , the groups R1 and R2 lie in a plane perpendicular to the plane containing R3 and R4 . Under these circumstances, the molecule is chiral (molecule whose arrangement of atoms is such that it cannot be superimposed on its mirror image) and can show optical activity.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: allometric growth
DEFN: A phenomenon in which parts and organs of the same organism grow at different rates. In other words, it is the growth in part of an organism in relation to its whole body. An example is legs increasing in length faster than the arms. It is studied by measuring the mass and size of any organ or part of the body and expressed in relation to the total mass or size of the entire organism using the relation Y = bx ⍺ , where Y is the mass of the organ, x is the mass of the organism, ⍺ is the coefficient of the organ and b is a constant. It is important in studying relative growth rates.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: allophane
DEFN: An amorphous hydrated aluminosilicate of variable composition and various colours such as blue, green, or yellow, with the chemical formula Al 2 O3 ·(SiO2 ) 1.3-2 ·(2.5-3)H2 O.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: allotrope
DEFN: An element, which exists in two or more different forms in the same physical state but, has the same chemical properties. One allotrope is more stable than another at a particular temperature and pressure. For example, diamond and graphite are allotropes of carbon. Normal dioxygen (O2 ) and ozone (O3 ) are allotropes of oxygen. Other elements, which exist in different allotropic forms are monoclinic and rhombic sulfur, red and white phosphorous and α -tin (grey tin) and β-tin (white tin). The existence of an element in more than one crystalline or molecular form is called allotropy .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alloxan
DEFN: A compound with molecular formula C4 H2 O4 N2 that is an important decomposition product of uric acid. It is obtained by oxidising uric acid with nitric acid. It can react with alkali to take up two molecules of water to produce urea and mesoxalic acid. It can be used experimentally to induce diabetes mellitus in animals by selectively destroying the insulin-secreting islet cells of the pancreas and therefore used in medical research.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alloy
DEFN: A metal mixed or blended with another metal or non-metal to give it special qualities such as resistance to corrosion and greater hardness, which none of the metals or non-metals alone has. The physical properties of an alloy depend on the metals chosen, the proportions combined and the process by which the alloy is formed. Examples of alloys are bronze, which is an alloy of copper and tin; steel, an alloy of iron and carbon and brass, which is an alloy of copper and zinc. WORD: allylic groups
DEFN: An allyl and derivatives formed by substitution. The term 'allylic position' or 'allylic site' refers to the saturated carbon atom. A group, such as -OH, attached at an allylic site is sometimes described as 'allylic'. An allyl group is a substituent with the structural formula H2 C=CH-CH2 R, where R is the connection to the rest of the molecule. It is made up of a methylene (-CH2 -), attached to a vinyl group (-CH=CH2 ).
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: allylic intermediates
DEFN: Carbanions, carbenium ions or radicals, formally derived by detachment of one hydron, hydride or hydrogen from the CH3 group of propene or derivatives thereof. An example is allyl cation (H2 C=CHCH2 + ).
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: almost all
DEFN: In mathematics, often written a.e. or p.p. , it is the truth set of all values with the exception of sets on zero measure. For example, if f ( x ) = 1 for all real x , g ( x ) = 1 for all irrational x and 0 for all rational x , then f and g agree almost everywhere, since the rationals have measure zero.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: almost perfect number
DEFN: The powers of 2, because the aliquot parts (proper divisors) of 2 n sum to 2 n^-1. The only known almost perfect numbers are 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, ..., which form the powers of 2.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alnico
DEFN: A trade name for a series of alloys containing iron, aluminium, nickel, cobalt and copper used to make permanent magnets.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alpha decay
DEFN: The disintegration of certain radioactive isotopes, causing the spontaneous emission of an alpha ( ) particle. An alpha particle is a stable particle formed from two protons and two neutrons and is identical to the nucleus of the helium atom. The general reaction for alpha decay is A Z X → A^-2 Z^-4 Y + 2 4 He , where X is the parent nuclide, Y is the daughter nuclide, Z is the atomic mass of X and A is the atomic number of X . The atomic number or the number of protons decreases by two and the mass number decreases by four as a result. Alpha decay commonly occurs in elements such as bismuth with atomic numbers greater than 83. It can occur in some rare-earth elements such as neodymium and lutetium with atomic numbers between 60 and 71.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alpha haemolysis/haemolysis
DEFN: A partial haemolysis (disintegration of red blood cells, with the release of haemoglobin) on blood agar plates (nutrient culture medium that is enriched with blood and used for the growth of certain strains of bacteria) as indicated by a green discolouration around the bacterial colony. It is caused by hydrogen peroxide produced by the bacterium, oxidising haemoglobin producing the green oxidised derivative, methemoglobin. Examples of α -haemolysis are Streptococcus pneumoniae that causes bacterial pneumonia and some other diseases; and Streptococcus viridans which causes dental infections some other diseases.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alpha helix
DEFN: The most common form of secondary structure of proteins in which the polypeptide chain is coiled into a helix. This is a right-handed coiled or spiral conformation, in which every backbone N-H group donates a hydrogen bond to the backbone C=O group of the amino acid four residues earlier. The -helix is the most regular and the most predictable from sequence, as well as the most prevalent.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alpha radiation
DEFN: A stream or rays of relatively low penetrating power alpha particles emitted by radium and other radioactive substances moving at high speed of up to one tenth of the speed of light (1/10 × 299 792 458 m/s) but having small masses. Alpha radiations or rays are the least penetrating of the three radiations (alpha, beta and gamma) and can be stopped or absorbed by a sheet of paper and can be slightly deflected by a strong magnetic or electric field. Although radiation can be harmful, it is also useful. One use of alpha radiation is in smoke detectors. For example, radioactive americium releases alpha radiation, which ionises the air inside a fire detector. Smoke from a fire absorbs alpha radiation, altering the ionisation and triggering an alarm. Alpha rays penetrate only a small distance in dense matter such as living tissue and have been used to cause local destruction of tissue such as in cancer therapy.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alpha-methyltryptamine
DEFN: A synthetic derivative of tryptamine with stimulant and hallucinogenic properties. It is used illegally as a club drug.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alpha-particle scattering
DEFN: The scattering of alpha particles by thin films of heavy metals, notably gold, a phenomenon explained by Ernest Rutherford in 1911. The patterns produced by such scattering offan extremely thin sheet of gold were early evidence that atoms contain a tiny, positively charged core, what is now called the atomic nucleus. This led to the development of the Rutherford model(planetary model) of the atom and eventually to the Bohr model. Rutherford scattering is also sometimes referred to as Coulomb scattering because it relies only upon static electric (Coulomb) forces and the minimal distance between particles is set only by this potential. Rutherford backscattering is any technique using high energy particles directed toward a sample, in which the bombarding particles are detected and recorded as function of energy and/or angle. The technique is mostly used for determining depth distributions of elements based on the energy of the backscattered particle. In general, He + or H + particles are used at energies in the order of 100 keV to some MeV.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alphametic
DEFN: A type of mathematical game consisting of a mathematical equation among unknown numbers, in which the letters represent distinct digits form related words or meaningful phrases. The goal is to identify the value of each letter. The game can be extended to puzzles that use non-alphabetic symbols instead of letters.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alteratives
DEFN: Herbs that gradually restore the proper function of the body and increase health and vitality; herbs which purify the blood. Herbs like these can be used for quite a long time for active cleansing of the blood and purification of vital organs such as the kidneys, liver, spleen and the bowel. Such herbs include corn silk and mahogany.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alternant
DEFN: 1. A conjugated system of π -electrons is termed alternant if its atoms can be divided into two sets so that no atom of one set is directly linked to any other atom of the same set. Alternatively, describing a conjugated molecule whose carbon atoms can be divided into two sets so that members of one set are formally bonded only to members of the other set. An example is naphthalene. 2. In mathematics, a determinant constructed from n functions and n points (not necessarily distinct) by taking the i , j th entry to be the value of the i th function at the j th point or vice versa. 3. Also known as disjunct , in logic, it is either of a pair of propositions or formulae related or operated upon by disjunction. Leaf Leaf stalk Main stem Alternate leaves- e.g., Rosa.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alternate angles
DEFN: Either pair of angles contained between two given lines and a transversal, when a transversal cuts or crosses the two lines. They are non-adjacent and are on the opposite sides of the transversal. Alternate angles are equal if and only if the lines are parallel. For example, in the diagram, angles a and b alternate, which means a is equal to b ( a = b ), so are c and d ( c = d ).
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alternating current
DEFN: Electric current that changes direction periodically. If a graph of the current against time is plotted it is in a wave form, with its value varying periodically around zero. AC can come in a number of forms, as long as the voltage and current are alternating. The most common type of AC is the sine wave. The AC in homes and offices have an oscillating voltage that produces a sine wave. There are three parts to a sine wave: amplitude, frequency and phase. Some other waveforms include ths square waves, often used in digital and switching electronics to test their operation; and triangle waves, found in sound synthesis and are useful for testing linear electronics like amplifiers. The frequency of alternating current (AC) is measured in hertz. Typical equations describing an AC current and AC voltage are: I = I O sin ωt = I O sin2 πft and V = V o sin ω t = V o sin2 π ft , where ωt = 2 π f and f = 1/ T , T is the period of the signal in seconds, f is the frequency in Hz, ωt is the angular velocity in radian per second, I and V are the instantaneous values of the current and voltage respectively and I O and V O are the peak current and voltage respectively. The frequency of the domestic supply is different in different parts of the world. In Europe and most parts of the world, it is 50 Hz and in the U.S., it is 60 Hz.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alternating group
DEFN: Any subgroup of the symmetric or permutation group on a set S consisting of all even permutations of n objects. It is denoted by A n , has order n !/2 and has index 2 in S n , the symmetric group of degree n . For n ≥ 5, A n is the only proper non-trivial normal subgroup of S n and is itself simple.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alternating multilinear function
DEFN: A multilinear form in which all underlying vector spaces are identical and the interchanging two variables merely changes the sign of the expression.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alternating series test
DEFN: A method used to prove that an alternating series with terms that decrease in absolute value is a convergent series; and to establish conditional convergence of an alternating series one need only to verify that the terms decrease monotonically to zero in absolute value. In this case the error after adding n terms is always less than the magnitude of the next. For example, 1 - 1 3 + 1 5 - 1 7 + 1 9 - ... is convergent, since a n = - 1 ( ) n 2 n + 1 = 2 n + 1 decreases monotonically to zero as n tends to infinity; its sum is π /4.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alternation theorem
DEFN: The result, for continuous functions g 1 , ..., g n on [ a , b ] that satisfy the Haar condition (as holds for 1, x , ..., x n ) , that in order for a generalised polynomial P to be the best approximation in Chebyshev norm to a continuous function f it is necessary and sufficient that the error function r = f - P possesses at least n + 1 alternations, points at which r x i ( ) =- r x i^-1 ( ) = ± r ∞ .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alternative complement pathway
DEFN: One of three pathways in the complement system of innate immunity capable of initiating the processes that result in C3 activation for the immune system's natural defence against pathogens and invading foreign agents. C3 is activated directly by pathogens, or by foreign materials and damaged tissues. Complement system of innate immunity is independent of antibody activity. It is part of the innate immune system and one of the major means by which the body recognises foreign agents and pathogens that may be harmful to it. C3-C9 are abbreviated versions of complement components 3 and 9 , which are plasma proteins of the innate immune system. The other two pathways in the complement system of immunity are: the c lassical pathway which is triggered by activation of the C1-complex; and the lectin pathway which is activated by the binding of MBL (Mannose-Binding Lectin), to carbohydrates on the surfaces of pathogens.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alternative hypothesis
DEFN: The hypothesis that sample observations are influenced by some non-random cause and do not conform with a given null hypothesis; the alternative is only accepted if the value of a test statistics is sufficient, at a chosen significance level, to reject the null. It is denoted by H1 or H a .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alternative theorem
DEFN: Any theorem stating that of two systems of equations or inequalities one or the other always has a solution.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: altocumulus clouds
DEFN: Clouds composed of flattened globules that may be arranged in groups. They are small, white, puffy clouds composed primarily of water droplets that sometimes slowly drift across the sky and forms at altitudes of between 2000 - 6000 metres above the ground with a vertical velocity of 0.05 ms^-1 to 0.1 m s^-1 . Altocumulus clouds usually form in a layer of moist air, where air currents undulate gently, like waves on the sea. As a wave rises, water vapour condenses and forms the cloud. In the wave troughs, water evaporates. There the cloud is thinner or the sky may be clear, producing the bands of cloud that are sometimes seen.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alum
DEFN: A group of double salts, with the chemical formula A2 SO4 . B2 (SO4 ) 3 .24H2 O, where A is a monovalent metal and B a trivalent metal, occurring naturally in various minerals and can be prepared and purified by crystallisation from their solutions. The most important alums are those of potassium sulfate (potassium alum or potash alum, K2 SO4 × Al 2 (SO4 ) 3 × 24H2 O), ammonium sulfate and sodium sulfate. Alternatively, it may be defined as the common name for aluminium potassium sulfate, which is a double salt whose crystals contain two different metal ions or any double sulfate of a monovalent metal or radical such as sodium, potassium or ammonium with a trivalent metal such as aluminium and iron. The commonest is the double sulfate of potassium and aluminium (aluminium potassium sulfate - (KAl(SO4 ) 2 .12H2 O) 2 , which is a white crystalline powder that is readily soluble in water. It is used for paper-sizing agents, flocculating agents in water treatment, mordants in dyeing and in pickles, baking powder, fire extinguishers and medicines.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alumina
DEFN: A family of inorganic compounds, with chemical formula Al 2 O3 , density 3.95-4.1 g/cm3 , melting point 2072 °C (2345 K) and boiling point 2977 °C (3250 K). It is a white powder, soluble in most alkalis or caustic, strong acids but insoluble in water. It is widely distributed in clays, slates and shale. It is used in the production of aluminium metal, as an abrasive due to its hardness and as a refractory material due to its high melting point, as well as for ceramics and electrical insulation.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aluminate
DEFN: A salt formed when aluminium hydroxide or γ -alumina is dissolved in solutions of strong bases, such as sodium hydroxide. Aluminates exist in solutions containing the aluminate ion, commonly written [Al(OH) 4 ] - . Examples of aluminate compounds include sodium aluminate (Na 2 Al 2 O4 ) and potassium aluminate (K2 Al 2 O4 ), both being alkali-ion precipitates of the tetrahedral monomer, obtained from an alkaline solution. Aluminate can also refer to the polyatomic (molecule that has more than two atoms) ion, for example, chlorate(ClO -.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aluminium
DEFN: The International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry's (IUPAC) name for hydrated aluminium oxide also known as aluminium hydrate , aluminium hydroxide and hydrated alumina with the chemical formula Al 2 O3 ·3H2 O or Al(OH) 3 .Al 2 O3 ·3H2 O or Al(OH) 3 . It is a white powder, which is insoluble in water but soluble in hydrochloric or sulfuric acid and sodium hydroxide. It is used in the manufacture of ceramic glasses and in paper coating.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aluminium chloride
DEFN: A white or yellowish deliquescent solid with the chemical formula AlCl 3 . It is the main compound of aluminium and chlorine. It has a low melting and boiling point. Aluminium chloride is hygroscopic and has a very high affinity for water. It fumes in moist air and hisses when mixed with liquid water as the Cl - ions are displaced with H2 O molecules in the lattice to form the hexahydrate (AlCl 3 ·6H2 O). It is used as a catalyst in the cracking of petroleum hydrocarbons. It is also a catalyst in certain other organic reactions, especially the Friedel-Crafts reaction.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aluminium ethanoate
DEFN: A white solid, with the chemical formula Al(OOCCH3 ) 3 , which can only be made in the absence of water. For example, ethanoic anhydride and aluminium chloride at 180 °C (453 K), decomposes on heating. It is slightly soluble in cold water and decomposes in warm water. It is used in dyeing as a mordant, particularly in combination with aluminium sulfate (red liquor); in the paper and board industry for sizing and hardening; and in tanning. It was previously used as an antiseptic and astringent.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aluminium hydroxide
DEFN: A white crystalline compound, with the chemical formula Al(OH) 3 , melting point 300 o C (573 K) and relative density 2.42-2.52. The compound occurs naturally as the mineral gibbsite (monoclinic). In the laboratory it can be prepared by precipitation from solutions of aluminium salts. It is used as a filtering medium and in ion-exchange and chromatography devices, as an antacid, as an adsorbent, emulsifier, mordant, in the manufacture of paper, ceramics, printing inks, detergents, for waterproofing fabrics and in dentrifrices and antiperspirants.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aluminium oxide
DEFN: A white crystalline amphoteric compound, which is the principal source of aluminium. It occurs in bauxite and its chemical formula is Al 2 O3 .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aluminium potassium sulfate
DEFN: A colourless or white crystalline compound with the chemical formula Al 2 (SO4 ) 3 .K2 SO4 .24H2 O, relative density 1.757, molar mass 474 g/ mol, melting point 92-93 °C (365-366 K) and boiling point 200 °C (473 K). It occurs naturally as the mineral kalinite salt and can be prepared by recrystallisation from a solution containing equimolar quantities of potassium sulfate and aluminium sulfate. It forms cubic or octahedral crystals that are soluble in cold water, very soluble in hot water but insoluble in ethanol and acetone. Its uses include as a mordant for dyeing and in the tanning and finishing of leather goods (for white leather).
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aluminium sulfate
DEFN: A white crystalline compound, with the chemical formula Al 2 (SO4 ) 3 . It occurs naturally in the rare mineral alunogenite (Al 2 (SO) 3 .18H2 O) and may be prepared by dissolving aluminium hydroxide or china clays (aluminosilicates) in sulfuric acid. It is used as a foaming agent in fire extinguishers, as a flocculating agent in water treatment and sewage works and as a mordant in dyeing.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aluminosilicate
DEFN: Any of colourless chemical compounds, with chemical formula 3Al 2 O3 ·2SiO2 , consisting of alumina and silica with various metals. They are usually found in minerals and rocks.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alunogenite
DEFN: A mineral form of hydrated aluminium sulfate with chemical formula Al 2 (SO4 ) 3 .18H2 O.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: alvarez, luis walter
DEFN: An American experimental physicist, who spent 42 years at the University of Carlifonia, Berkely (1936 - 1978). After working on radar and the atomic bomb during World War II, he concentrated on particle physics. In 1938, he discovered that some radioactive elements decay, when an orbital electron merges with the atom's nucleus, producing an element with an atomic number smaller by one, a form of beta decay. In 1939, together with Felix Bloch (1905 - 83), they made the first measurement of the magnetic moment of the neutron. In 1959, he built the first large bubble chamber and developed the technique for using it to study charged particles, for which he was awarded the 1958 Nobel Prize for physics.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: am0 sunlight
DEFN: Solar irradiance in space just above the atmosphere of the Earth on a plane perpendicular to the direction of the Sun. In other words, it is the spectrum outside the atmosphere. AM is the acronym for air mass , which is the optical path length through the Earth's atmosphere for light from a celestial source. Air mass normally indicates relative air mass, the path length relative to that at the zenith at sea level. The value of the air mass is often given by appending its value to AM, so that AM1 indicates an air mass of 1, AM2 indicates an air mass of 2, etc. On average the extraterrestrial irradiance is 1367 Watts/metre 2 (W/m2 ). This value varies by ±3% as the Earth orbits the Sun. The Earth's closest approach to the Sun occurs around January 4th and it is furthest from the Sun around July 5th. Solar cells used for space power applications, such as those on communications satellites are generally characterised by AM0.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amdahl's law
DEFN: In computer programming, it states that in a program with parallel processing, relatively few instructions that have to be performed in sequence will have a limiting factor on program speedup such that adding more processors may not make the program run faster.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: americium
DEFN: A silvery, shiny radioactive metallic transuranic element belonging to the actinoids, with the chemical symbol Am and atomic number 95, melting point 1176 o C (1449 K), boiling point 2011°C (2284 K), density 12 g cm^-3 and the mass number of the most stable isotope is 243. It was discovered in 1944. It is obtained from the beta decay of plutonium and it is a source of alpha particle used in research. It is also commonly used in smoke alarms.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amicable numbers
DEFN: Two different numbers so related that the sum of the proper divisors of each is equal to the other. For example, the pair of numbers 220 and 284 are said to be amicable numbers in the sense that the sum of the proper positive divisors of each, sum to the other. For 220 ⇒ 1 + 2 + 4 + 5 + 10 + 11 + 20 + 22 + 44 + 55 + 110 = 284; and for 284 ⇒ 1 + 2 + 4 + 71 + 142 = 220.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amides
DEFN: 1. Any organic chemical derived from carboxylic acids by the replacement of the hydroxyl group (-OH) with an amino group (-NH2 ). The O=C-N group in an amide forms the amide group. Chalcogen replacement analogues are called thio-, seleno- or telluro- amides. Compounds having one, two or three alkyl groups on a given nitrogen atom may be designated as primary, secondary and tertiary amides, respectively. The three simplest amides are methanamide (carbamaldehyde or formamide, HCONH2 ), ethanamide (acetamide, CH3 CONH2 ) and propanamide (CH3 CH2 CONH2 ), which are known as primary amides. Referring to , whose structure is shown in and tertiary amides (RCONR2 ) are named by using an upper case N to designate that the alkyl group(s) is/are on the nitrogen atom. Alkyl groups attached to the nitrogen are named as substituents. The letter N is used to indicate they are attached to the nitrogen. 2. Metal derivatives of ammonia and amines, in which a cation replaces a hydrogen atom on nitrogen. Such compounds are also called azanides, for example, lithium diisopropylamide. WORD: amidines
DEFN: Derivatives of oxoacids R n E(=O)OH in which the hydroxy group is replaced by an amino group and the oxo group is replaced by =NR. Amidines include carboxamidines, sulfinamidines and phosphinamidines, R2 P(=NR)NR2 . In organic chemistry an unspecified amidine is commonly a carboxamidine.

WORD: amidrazones

WORD: aminals
DEFN: Compounds having two amino groups attached to the same carbon, R2 C(NR2 ) 2 .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amine
DEFN: Any of a class of organic chemical compounds in which one or more of the hydrogen atoms of ammonia have been replaced by other groups of atoms such as an alkyl or aryl group. The four categories of amine include the following: (i) Primary amines with the general structural formula RNH2 have two hydrogen atoms and one alkyl or aryl group attached to the nitrogen to form the amine or amino group -NH2 . Examples of primary alliphatic amines include methylamine ( aminomethane ) , ethylamine ( aminoethane ) , propylamine , 2-aminopropane and butylamine. In primary aromatic amines, the amino or amine group is directly attached to the aromatic benzene ring. Examples include phenylamineoraniline , 1 , 3 - diaminobenzeneand 2-methylphenylamine. (ii) Secondary amines , with the general structural formula R2 NH have one hydrogen atom and two alkyl or aryl groups attached to the nitrogen (N) or no hydrogen if one of the substituent bonds is double. Examples of secondary aliphatic amines include dimethylamine, diethylamine, dipropylamine, ethylpropylamine and methylethanolamine; examples of secondary aromatic amines include diphenylamine, N-methylphenylamine and N-ethylphenylamine. (iii) Tertiary amines with the general structural formula R3 N have all three hydrogen atoms replaced by organic substituents. Examples of aliphatic tertiary amines include trimethylamine, ethyldimethylamine and diethylmethylamine. Examples of aromatic tertiary amines include N,N-dimethylphenylamine, N,N-diethylphenylamine and triphenylamine. (iv) Cyclic amines are either secondary or tertiary amines. Examples include the 3-member ring aziridine and the six- membered ring piperidine. N-methylpiperidine and N-phenylpiperidine are examples of cyclic tertiary amines. (v) Quaternary amine results when a fourth hydrogen is added and has a positive charge on the nitrogen atom. Examples include tetramethylammonium chloride and tetrapropylammonium chloride.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amine oxides
DEFN: Compounds derived from tertiary amines by the attachment of one oxygen atom to the nitrogen atom R3 N + -O - . It also includes the analogous derivatives of primary and secondary amines. Examples are pyridine N -oxide, a water-soluble crystalline solid and N -methylmorpholine N -oxide, which is an oxidant. Amine oxides are surfactants commonly used in consumer products such as shampoos, conditioners, detergents and hard surface cleaners.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aminiumyl radical ions
DEFN: Radical cations, R3 N .+ derived from trialkylammonium ions, R3 NH + by removal of a hydrogen atom. Aminiumyl radical ions are, except for H3 N .+ , synonymous with the ammoniumyl radical ions.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amino acid
DEFN: A class of organic compounds with the general formula R-CH(NH2)COOH, in which a carbon atom has bonds to an amino group (-NH2), a carboxyl group (-COOH), a hydrogen atom (-H) and an organic side chain (-R). Amino acids are the building blocks of proteins. There are 20 standard amino acids that make up all proteins in living organisms.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aminyl radicals
DEFN: The nitrogen-centred radical H2 N . , formally derived by the removal of a hydrogen atom from ammonia and its hydrocarbyl derivatives R2 N.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: ammeter
DEFN: An instrument used to measure the current flowing through an electric circuit. The main types include moving coil ammeter, moving iron ammeter and the thermo ammeter. Ammeters, as well as voltmeters and ohmmeters, are designed with the use of a sensitive current detector such as a galvanometer. An ammeter is always connected in series in a circuit. When a resistor with a low internal resistance is connected across (parallel) a galvanometer, it can be used as a current measuring instrument. The low internal resistance resistor serves as a by pass (shunt) and allows most of the current to pass through so that the desired current flows through the meter at its full-scale deflection. WORD: ammonia
DEFN: A colourless, pungent smelling gas, with the chemical formula NH3 , melting point^-77.73°C (195 K) and boiling point^-33.34°C (240 K), which is lighter than air and forms a minute proportion of the atmosphere. It is very soluble in water, giving an alkaline solution. It turns moist red litmus paper blue and forms white fume with concentrated hydrochloric acid. It is found in volcanic gases and as a product of decomposition of animal and vegetable matter. It may be produced from coal or by the Haber- Bosch process. It is used to make fertilisers and nitric acid. Heat Card cover Preparation of NH3 Calcium oxide to dry the ammonia Ammonia Calcium hydroxide and ammonium chloride.

WORD: ammonium
DEFN: The univalent ion, NH4 + or positively charged polyatomic group with the chemical formula NH4 . It is formed by the reaction of ammonia (NH3 ) with an acid.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: ammonium carbonate
DEFN: A white crystalline solid, with the chemical formula (NH4 ) 2 CO3 , density 1.50 g/cm3 , melting point 58°C (331 K), decomposes at a boiling point and readily sublimes at room temperature and breaks down slowly into its constituent gases: ammonia, carbon dioxide and water. It is manufactured by heating a mixture of ammonium chloride and calcium carbonate and recovering the product as a sublimed solid. It is used in dyeing, wool preparation and in baking powder.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: ammonium chloride
DEFN: A volatile salt with the chemical formula NH4 Cl that forms white crystals around volcanic craters. It is prepared synthetically for use as electrolytes in dry-cell batteries, as flux in soldering, tinning and galvanising and as fertilisers and dyes.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: ammonium hydrogen carbonate
DEFN: A white crystalline compound, with the chemical formula NH4 HCO2 , formed naturally as a decay product of nitrogenous matter and is made commercially by various methods including the action of carbon dioxide and steam on a solution of ammonium carbonate, heating commercial ammonium carbonate and the interaction of ammonia, carbon dioxide and water vapour. It is used in baking powders and medicines.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: ammonium hydroxide
DEFN: An alkali, with the chemical formula NH4 OH which is made by dissolving ammonia in water. The solution contains hydrates of ammonia. It is used in making soaps and fertilisers. Although its name suggests a salt with composition [NH4 + ][OH - ], it is not actually possible to isolate samples of NH4 OH.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: ammonium nitrate
DEFN: A colourless, crystalline solid with the chemical formula NH4 NO3 , density 1.725 g/cm3 , melting point 169.6 °C (442.75 K), which decomposes at 210 °C (483 K) and is prepared by the neutralisation of nitric acid with ammonia. It is used in fertilisers, herbicides, insecticides and explosives.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: ammonium phosphate
DEFN: A colourless, crystalline compound with chemical formula (NH4 ) 3 PO4 , which is obtained from mixing concentrated solutions of ammonia and phosphoric acid or the addition of excess of ammonia to the acid phosphate (NH4 ) 2 (HPO4 ). It is used as fertiliser and fire retardant.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: ammonium sulfate
DEFN: A white crystalline compound, with the chemical formula (NH4 ) 2 SO4 , which is used as fertiliser and for water purification.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: ammonium thiocyanate
DEFN: A colourless, water-soluble crystalline compound with chemical formula NH4 SCN, density 1.305 g/cm3 , melting point 149.5 °C (423 K), which decomposes at 170 °C (443 K). It is made from ammonia and carbon sulfide in ethanol. On heating, it turns into its isomer thiourea, SC(NH2 ) 2 . Its solutions give a characteristic blood-red colour with iron (III) compounds and so are employed as a test for ferric iron. It is used as a rapid fixative in photography and as an ingredient in making explosives.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amnion
DEFN: The innermost of the three membranes in mammals which form a sac that enclose the foetus within the uterus. It contains the amniotic fluid which helps to cushion the embryo. A the graphite lattice as well as to the diamond lattice. In mineralogy, amorphous carbon is the name used for coal, soot and other impure forms of the element or carbon that are neither graphite nor diamond. In a crystallographic, however, these materials are not truly amorphous, but are polycrystalline or nanocrystalline materials of graphite or diamond within an amorphous carbon matrix.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amoled
DEFN: A type of organic light-emitting diode (OLED) used in screen displays consisting of a thin film transistor of an organic substance, often thin films of polymer sandwiched between two conductors that emits light when an electric current is applied. AMOLED has a storage capacitor that makes the line pixels light all the time allowing for higher resolution and larger display sizes. The screen is lighter, thinner, and durable. Amoled screen technology has been used in smartphones, television screens, computer monitors, etc. Another type of organic light-emitting diode (OLED) is the passive-matrix organic light-emitting diode (PMOLED) that does not contain a storage capacitor and so the pixels in each line are actually offmost of the time and needs more power to make them brighter.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amount concentration
DEFN: Amount of a constituent divided by the volume of the mixture. For entities B it is often denoted by [ B ]. In other words, it is the number of moles contained or dissolved in one cubic decimetre of a solution. The common unit is mole per cubic decimetre (mol dm^-3 ) or mole per litre (mol L^-1 ). Its symbol is c , sometimes denoted by M .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amount of substance
DEFN: The amount of chemical or elementary entities or pure substance (atoms, molecules, ions or electrons) containing the same number of chemical units as there are atoms in 12g of carbon^-12. The SI unit of amount of substance is the mole. Its symbol is n .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: ampere
DEFN: The SI unit of electric current, with the symbol A. One ampere, is defined as the constant current that, if maintained in two straight parallel conductors of infinite length and negligible circular cross-section and placed 1 metre apart in vacuum, would produce between these conductors a force of 2 × 10 -7 newton per metre. One ampere is equal to 6.28 x 10 18 electrons flowing around a circuit each second. A current of about 0.25 ampere lights a 60 watt bulb in a table lamp. It measures the rate of flow of a charge. It is named after the French physicist André Marie Ampère (1775-1836).
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: ampere's law
DEFN: A law used to determine the magnetic flux density B and the distance r from a conductor of length l, carrying a current I. It is of the form dB = ( µ ο I sin θ dl )/ πr 2 where dB is the infinitesimal element of the magnitude of the magnetic flux density at a distance r at point P from the element length dl of a conductor, µ 0 is the magnetic permeability of free space, l is the length of the line joining the element of the conductor and P.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amperometric titration
DEFN: A method of determining the chemical composition of a solution by measuring the current passing through a cell containing the solution. The potential is held constant during titration for both the indicator and reference electrodes with changes in the current being measured. The current flowing through the cell is measured as a function of the substance being titrated.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amphetamine
DEFN: A colourless, volatile compound, 2-amino^-3-phenyl propane or a derivative of this compound, with chemical formula C9 H13 N, that stimulates the central nervous system by causing the release of the transmitters noradrenaline and dopamine from nerve endings. It inhibits sleep, suppresses appetite and has variable effects on mood. Its prolonged use can lead to addiction.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amphiprotic
DEFN: Self-ionising solvent, which possesses both characteristics of Brønsted acids and bases. Examples are water (H2 O) and methanol or wood alcohol (CH3 OH), in contrast to aprotic solvent.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amphoteric oxide
DEFN: A substance such as aluminium oxide (Al 2 O3 ) and zinc oxide (ZnO), which can behave both as a basic and acidic oxide. For example, when aluminium oxide Amplexicaul leaf (Al 2 O3 ) reacts with hydrogen chloride (HCl), it acts as a base to form the salt aluminium chloride (AlCl 3 ). When reacted with sodium hydroxide (NaOH), it acts as an acid to form sodium aluminate (NaAlO2 ).

WORD: amplifier
DEFN: In electronics, it is a device that magnifies or increases the strength of a signal, such as a radio signal or sound. A sine wave (voltage) applied to the input terminal produces a much larger sine wave signal across the output signals. The output signal is inverted compared with the input signal. When the input voltage (signal) is at a peak, the output voltage (signal) is at a trough and vice versa. Amplifiers are classified by the size of the signal (large or small), its physical configuration and how it processes the input signal (how the input signal and current flowing in the load relates). All amplifiers provide a certain amount of gain or amplification factor. It is the relationship that exists between the signal measured at the output with the signal measured at the input and is usually defined as the mean ratio of the signal output of a system to the signal input of the same system. It is usually expressed by the logarithmic decibel (dB) units. The three different kinds of amplifier gains are voltage gain, current gain and power gain. A gain may be linear or non-linear. In a linear gain, the output signal strength varies in direct proportion to the input signal strength and delivers more power to a load. In a nonlinear amplification, a change in input does not produce a proportional change in output. The four basic types of amplifiers include the following: (i) voltage amplifier, which converts a small input voltage into a much larger output voltage, hence an increased power. It is used to increase signals, for example, to boost an audio signal to speakers. (ii) current amplifier, which converts a small input current into a much larger output current; (iii) transconductance amplifier or operational transconductance amplifier, which converts an input voltage to an output current. Its applications include as variable frequency oscillators, variable gain amplifiers and current-controlled filters. (iv) Transimpedance or transimpedance amplifier converts an input current to an output voltage. It is used to convert low-level photodiode currents to usable voltage signals. In the diagram, R1 and R2 are potential dividers that provide the necessary R1 base-bias; R L is the load that produces the output across AB; C1 is the capacitor that isolates the d.c. component in the input signal from the current; C2 is the large capacitor that prevents undesirable feedback of the amplified signal to the base-emmitter circuit; and R3 is the emitter resistance that stabilises the circuit for excessive temperature rise. As the collector current rises, the potential A difference between the emitter and base lowers and the collector current is automatically lowered.

WORD: amplitude
DEFN: 1. The farthest distance that a vibrating or oscillating system such as a pendulum travels from a mean or equilibrium point; or the maximum displacement of a wave, for example, a sine wave from its mean position during a cycle. Since the sine function varies from +1 to^-1, the amplitude is 1. A sine wave is given by the formula a sin( ωt ), where a is the amplitude. The energy associated with either a simple harmonic oscillation or a waveform is proportional to the square of the amplitude. With sound waves, the greater the amplitude, the louder is the sound. 2. In mathematics, it is also known as argument , anomaly or azimuth , it is the angle that describes the direction of a complex number when it is plotted in a complex plane using polar coordinates. It is measured in radians as an angle in standard position. Peak-to-peak value Mean position amplitude Amplitude of a sinusoid or oscillating system.

WORD: amu unit
DEFN: A unit used to indicate mass on an atomic or molecular scale. Its value is precisely one-twelfth the mass of an atom of carbon^-12, which has six protons and six neutrons in its nucleus.
