WORD: amygdalin
DEFN: A bitter crystalline compound with molecular formula C20 H27 O11 N, which is a glucoside found in bitter almonds, cherry kernels, apricots and other fruits and vegetable products.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amyl alcohol
DEFN: Any of eight isomers of the alcohol composition with the chemical formula C5 H11 OH but different structures. It is synthesised from hydrocarbons or extracted from fusel oil. Mixtures of these compounds are used as solvents for resins and oily materials and in the manufacture of other chemicals, especially amyl acetate, a solvent for nitrocellulose lacquers. Commercial amyl alcohol, a mixture of these isomers, has a sharp odour and is used as a solvent.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amyl compound
DEFN: An organic compound containing the amyl group C5 H11 , which has eight isomers. Amyl compounds include: (i) amyl alcohols , synthesised from hydrocarbons or extracted from fusel oil and used as a solvent; (ii) isoamyl nitrite (C5 H11 ONO), a vasodilator used to dilate the blood vessels of patients with some forms of heart disease such as angina; (iii) amyl acetate also known as banana oil with a pleasant, fruity smell and used as a solvent for nitrocellulose and as a flavouring for candy.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amyl group
DEFN: Any of the isomeric arrangement of the monovalent alkyl group, with the chemical formula C5 H11 -.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amyl nitrite
DEFN: A pale brown volatile liquid organic compound, with chemical formula C5 H11 ONO, which is normally used in medicine to dilate the blood vessels of patients with some forms of heart disease such as angina.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: amylum
DEFN: The white, granular, polysaccharide with the general chemical formula (C6 H10 O5 ) n , where n may range from 100 to several thousands. It is manufactured in the green leaves of all green plants from excess glucose produced during photosynthesis and serves the plant as a reserve food supply. It is used by humans and animals to produce energy, thus forming the most important component in their diet. The starch is broken down into its constituent sugar molecules, which then supply energy to the tissues. In the dark the starch thus formed is rapidly broken down to sucrose and transported to other organs. It is contained in large amounts in plants such as the roots of cassava ( Manihot esculenta ), which yield tapioca, the rhizomes of arrowroot ( Maranta arundinacea ), the stem pith of the some sago palms such as species of Metroxylon and Arenga, the tubers of potato and the grains of various cereals, especially maize, wheat, rice and sorghum. A Starch is processed to produce many of the sugars in commercial foods. It can be used as a thickening, stiffening or gluing agent. It is also used as adhesive in the papermaking industries.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: anaerobic digestion
DEFN: It is a series of biological processes in which diverse microorganisms break down bio-degradable materials in the absence of oxygen, using the process of fermentation to break down organic matter from animals, plants or sewage to produce biogas, with the process taking place within a centralized system in a unit called an anaerobic digester, biogas reactor or biodigestor. An anaerobic digester is a unit designed for processing organics through the process of anaerobic decomposition. They take manure, sludge and other organic waste materials and produce methane (natural gas) fuel. From the foregoing, it is clear that the process of anaerobic digestion is used for industrial or domestic purposes to manage waste or produce fuel.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: analogue
DEFN: 1. A quantity which changes continuously. By contrast a digital quantity varies in series of distinct steps. 2. A term which describes a signal that is wavy in form rather than being pulsed (digital). At any moment it can have any value in the range allowed, rather than only a few. 3. A way of recording values by means of a continuous wave or motion. Examples are given by the hands of a clock and the typical speedometer in a motor vehicle. The pendulum clock is an analogue clock for measuring time.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: analogue-to-digital converter
DEFN: A type of integrated circuit that converts continuously varying analogue signals from instruments and sensors that monitor conditions, such as sound, movement and temperature into binary code or digital signal. The basic conversion steps include the following: An analogue input signal is converted from some continuously varying physical value such as pressure in air or frequency or wavelength of light, into a sequence of digital values, called samples at regular intervals. The amplitude at each interval is quantised or given a value and the values are mapped into a series of binary digits or bits. Coding is the process of converting the quantised signals into a digital representation, which is transmitted as a digital signal to the receiver, where it is decoded. Once the digital signals are decoded and processed, they are converted back to an analogue signal and transmitted to a device such as a speaker, which requires an analogue signal. ADCs are found in digital desk phones and cell phones, digital cameras, camcorders or scanners.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: analogy
DEFN: 1. Equivalence in biological function between body parts or organs that have appeared independently in different plants and animals. 2. A general similarity between two problems or methods, used to indicate the results of one problem from the known results of the other.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: analyser
DEFN: 1. A device, used in the polarisation of light, that is placed in the eyepiece of a polarimeter to observe plane-polarised light. The analyser, which may be a Nicol prism or Polaroid, can be oriented in different directions to investigate in which plane an incoming wave is polarised or if the light is plane polarised. If the analyser is horizontal when extinction of light takes place, the polarisation of light must have been in the vertical plane. The intensity of a beam of light transmitted through an analyser is proportional to cos2θ, where θ is the angle between the plane of polarisation and the plane of the analyser. Extinction is said to be produced by 'crossing' the polariser and analyser. 2. The left cerebral hemisphere of the brain that, in sport, is concerned with learning new skills, correcting flaws in technique and developing strategy.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: analysis
DEFN: 1. The process of breaking down a complex topic or substance into its constitute parts to learn what they do and how they relate to one another. This is used commonly by scientists in studies to understand complex things. For example, chemists may use qualitative analysis to identify the essential components of a particular chemical compound; use quantitative analysis to identify the proportions of components in a substance, or to break down chemical processes and examine the chemical reactions between elements of matter. In biological science, for example, blood may be examined in a laboratory to analysis its composition. Data Analysis is the process of collecting and organising data so that statistical and logical techniques can be used to answer questions, solve problems, and derive important information. 2. A branch of mathematics based on convergence and limits of continuous functions. It is derived out of calculus, with fields such as classical and complex analysis and more abstract functional analysis and the theory of linear operators. Examples are series, logarithmic functions, trigonometrical functions and calculus.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: analytic
DEFN: 1. In complex function, also known as regular or holomorphic , it is a function that is single-valued and differentiable at every point of its domain and in consequence possessing derivatives of all orders and agreeing with its Taylor series locally. The function may not be differentiable everywhere, it is only analytic at points where the function is differentiable. For example, exp z is analytic on the complex plane and log z is analytic on C\ (- ∞ , 0). 2. Also known as real analytic , of a real function, possessing derivatives of all orders and agreeing with its Taylor series locally. 3. In logic, of a proposition, true by virtue of the meanings of the words alone without reference to the facts; alternatively, true or false by virtue of meaning alone.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: analytic continuation
DEFN: 1. The construction of an analytic function whose restriction to a given domain is a given analytic function. 2. A procedure to extend the domain of a given analytic function. For example, sin z on the complex plane is the analytic continuation of sin z on the real line, since there is no other analytic function on C of which the restriction to the reals is the sine function.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: analytic function
DEFN: A complex function that is single-valued and differentiable at every point of its domain and in consequence possessing derivatives of all orders and agreeing with its Taylor series locally. The function may not be differentiable everywhere, it is only analytic at points where the function is differentiable. For example, exp z is holomorphic on the complex plain and log z is holomorphic on C\ (- ∞ , 0).

WORD: angle
DEFN: A measure of the turn or rotation between two lines or planes that meet; it is the inclination of one line relative to another. It can also be defined as a pair of rays that share a common end point but do not lie on the same line. The magnitude of an angle can be given in one of two units. The degree , with the symbol ° is one of the units often used and it is defined as one complete rotation divided by 360 o . The other unit is the radian , a standard unit of angular Angle Radius Arc Length θ o B A Angle (θ) in radians = Radius (OA) Arc Length (AB) Angle measure, used in many areas of mathematics. Its symbol is the rad. Radian can be defined as a unit of measurement of angle equal to 57.3 o , equivalent to the angle subtended at the centre of a circle by an arc equal in length to the radius. In other words, radian is a unit of measurement of angle that is equivalent to the angle subtended at the centre of a circle by an arc equal in length to the radius. One radian is equal to 57.2958 degrees. In the diagram, the angle θ in radians = arc length divided by radius of the circle: θ = AB / OA . An angle's measurement in radians is numerically equal to the length of a corresponding arc of a unit circle. Another unit, the steradian , with the symbol sr or squared radian is the SI unit of solid angle. It is used in three-dimensional space and functions. A right angle has exactly 90° (or π /2 radians), an acute angle is less than 90°, an obtuse angle measures more than 90° but less than 180° and a straight angle has exactly 180°. Any angle that is at most 180° is called a convex angle . Any angle that is at most 180° is called convex angle . An angle between 180° and 360° is called a reflex angle . An angle of exactly 360° is a complete turn or one revolution. Complementary angles add to give 90°, supplementary angles make a total of 180°. In spherical geometry, a spherical angle is that formed by intersecting arcs of two great circles. Its magnitude is equal to that of the angle between the two planes of the great circles.

WORD: angle of friction
DEFN: The angle of a plane to the horizontal when a body placed on the plane will just start to slide. It is given by " λ ", which is the angle tan λ = μ s , where μ s is the coefficient of static friction.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: angle strain
DEFN: The tension that develops in a molecule when bond angles deviate from the normal value. For example, in cyclopropane, C5 H10, the angles between the bonds of the ring differ from the normal tetrahedral angle of 109° 28', which is found in cyclohexane, leading to angle strain in the molecule. The presence of angle strain in a molecule indicates that in a specific chemical conformation there exist bond angles that deviate from the ideal bond angles required to achieve maximum bond strength.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: anglesite
DEFN: A mineral form of lead (II) sulfate, with the chemical formula PbSO4 , which is usually formed by the oxidation of galena. Due to the fact that it does not occur in large enough quantity, it is of no particular commercial value.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: angular acceleration
DEFN: The rate of change of angular velocity with respect to time. In SI units, it is measured in radians per second squared (rad/s 2 ). Its symbol is α (alpha). The average angular acceleration of an object whose angular velocity changes from ω 1 to ω 2 over time t is (ω 2 - ω 1 )/ t . Instantaneous acceleration is the value approached as t becomes smaller.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: angular magnification
DEFN: The ratio of the angle subtended by the image at the eye to the angle subtended by the object at the eye. It is represented by angular magnification, M θ = θ 1 θ 0 , where θ 0 is the angular size of an object and θ 1 is the angular size of the image after passing through an optical system.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: angular mass
DEFN: A measure of an object's resistance to changes to its rotation, which is the sum of all the products formed by multiplying the magnitude of each element of mass by the square of its distance from the axes. Its symbol is I. The moment of inertia of an object about a given axis describes how difficult it is to change its angular motion about that axis. If a body A that ω is the fundamental frequency of the oscillator, then as a first approximation, the frequency shift ∆ω=ω-ω o is proportional to the square of the oscillation amplitude A. That is, ∆ω ∝ A2 . In a system of oscillators with natural frequencies ω α , ω β ..... Anharmonicity results in additional oscillations with frequencies ω α ± ω β . Anharmonicity also modifies the profile of the resonance curve, leading to interesting phenomena such as the foldover effect and superharmonic resonance. A harmonic oscillator obeys Hooke's Law and is an idealised expression that assumes that a system displaced from equilibrium responds with a restoring force whose magnitude is proportional to the displacement. Anharmonic oscillators exhibit chaotic or nonlinear behaviour. Some examples of anharmonic oscillators are the large-angle pendulum, which exhibits chaotic behaviour as a result of its anharmonicity; nonequilibrium semiconductors that possess a large hot carrier population, which exhibit nonlinear behaviours of various types related to the effective mass of the carriers; and ionospheric plasmas, which also exhibit nonlinear behaviour based on the anharmonicity of the plasma.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: angular momentum
DEFN: The momentum that a body has as a result of its rotation about an axis or the momentum of a body, calculated as the product of its mass and its angular velocity or the product of the angular velocity of a body and its moment of inertia about the axis of rotation. Its symbol is L . The angular momentum, of an orbiting body of mass, m , travelling at a velocity, V , along a circular orbit of radius, r , is given by L = mvr . Angular momentum is conserved; in that if the radius of the orbit is increased, the other values such as velocity will also increase so as to compensate the effect and maintain, the value of angular momentum.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: angular size
DEFN: The angle subtended by the observer by a diameter of a distant spherical body which is perpendicular to the line between the observer and the centre of the body. The angle may be measured in units of radians or in units of degrees, arc minutes (1 degree = 60 arc minutes) and arc seconds (one arc minute = 60 arc seconds). For example, the angular size of the moon in the sky is 30 arc minutes. The human eye cannot resolve details smaller than a few arc minutes.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: angular speed
DEFN: It is the magnitude of the angular velocity or the speed element of angular velocity. The average angular speed ( w ) is obtained by dividing the angular distance through which the body rotates by the time taken, expressed mathematically as w = θ/ t , where θ is the angular distance and t the time taken in seconds.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: angular velocity
DEFN: A vector quantity, which is the rate of angular displacement with respect to time. It specifies the angular speed or the rotational speed of an object and the axis about which the object rotates. The SI unit of angular velocity is radians per second (rad s^-1 ). It may also be measured in degrees, radians or revolutions per unit time. Its symbol is ω (omega).

WORD: annihilation
DEFN: 1. In radiochemistry, it is an interaction between a particle and its antiparticle in which they both disappear. 2. In physics, it is the process in which the entire mass of two colliding particles, one of matter and one of antimatter, is converted into radiant energy (according to Einstein's famous formula $$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$E = mc^2$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$$ ) in the form of gamma rays or mesons. It occurs within when a particle meets in anti-particle. For example, in the case of a collision between an electron and a positron (anti-electron), which have a combined rest mass of 1.02 MeV, the result is two gamma rays each with a characteristic energy of 0.511 MeV. When nucleons annihilate each other the result is mesons. Electron-positron annihilation is made use of in PET scanning, where the source of each pair of gamma photons can be traced allowing an image to be constructed that shows where a positron-emitting tracer is located in a patient's body.

WORD: anomalous expansion of water
DEFN: The unusual characteristic of expansion of water as it is cooled from 4 °C (277 K) to 0 °C (273 K), contrary to the fact that most substances contract when they are cooled. Water therefore has a greater density at 4 °C than ice at 0 °C, enabling ice to float in water. The water at the bottom of a pond in winter is warmer than that at the surface; making ponds and lakes freeze slowly from the surface downwards and usually remaining liquid near the bottom, where aquatic life is more likely to survive. Vol./m3 θ/°C kgm^-3 θ/ o.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: anomalous magnetic moment
DEFN: The difference between the observed gyromagnetic ratio of the electron and the value of exactly two predicted by Dirac's theory of the electron. The discrepancy is resolved using quantum electrodynamics.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: anomers
DEFN: The sugar isomers that differ in configuration about the anomeric carbon, i.e., the C1 atom of an aldose or the C2 atom of a ketose. The - and β-forms of glucose are examples of anomers.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: anorthite
DEFN: A mineral occurring in igneous rocks, consisting of calcium aluminium silicate with the chemical formula CaAl 2 Si 2 O8 , Mohs scale hardness 6, specific gravity 2.76 and melting point 1550 °C (1823 K). It occurs in the form of vitreous white or grey crystals. Anorthite is one of the end-members of the feldspar group.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antenna directivity
DEFN: The ratio of the maximum sensitivity of the power radiated per unit solid angle, to the average power radiated per unit solid angle. It varies directly as the effective aperture of the antenna and inversely as the square of the wavelength.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: anthracene
DEFN: A white, glittering, crystalline, aromatic hydrocarbon, with the chemical formula C14 H10 , melting point 215.8 °C (488.95 K) and boiling point 341.4 °C (614.55 K). It consists of three fused benzene rings and shows a faint blue fluorescence when pure. Anthracene is obtained from the fractional distillation of coal-tar. It is mainly used in the manufacture of dyes.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: anthraquinone
DEFN: A yellowish crystalline diketone, with the chemical formula C6 H4 (CO) 2 C6 H4 , density 1.308 g/cm3 , melting point 286 °C (559 K) and boiling point 379.8 °C (652.95 K). It is used in the manufacture of dyes.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: anti-
DEFN: 1. A prefix for the inverse of a function. For example, antitrigonometric is the inverse of the trigonometric function. 2. In the representation of stereochemical relationships 'anti' means 'on opposite sides' of a reference plane, in contrast to 'syn' which means 'on the same side', as in the following examples: (i) Two substituents attached to atoms joined by a single bond are antiif the torsion angle (dihedral angle) between the bonds to the substituents is greater than 90° or syn if it is less than 90°. (ii) In the older literature the terms antiand syn were used to designate stereoisomers of oximes and related compounds. That usage was superseded by the terms 'trans' and 'cis' or E and Z , respectively. (iii) When the terms are used in the context of chemical reactions or transformations, they designate the relative orientation of substituents in the substrate or product.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antiaromatic compounds
DEFN: Compounds that contain 4n (n ≠ 0) π -electrons in a cyclic planar or nearly planar, system of alternating single and double bonds, for example, cyclobuta^-1,3-diene.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antiaromaticity
DEFN: Those cyclic molecules with a conjugated pi electron system. The molecule must have 4n pi electrons, where n is any integer. Antiaromatic compounds are unstable and highly reactive than that of similar nonaromatic species. An example of antiaromatic compound is cyclobuta^-1,3-diene. Antiaromatic ones are prone to reactions causing changes in their structural type and display tendency to alternation of bond lengths and fluxional behaviour both in solution and in the solid. In antiaromatic molecules, an external magnetic field induces a paramagnetic electron current.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antibaryon
DEFN: The antiparticle of a baryon having a baryon number of B = -1.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antidepressant
DEFN: A drug such as tricyclic compounds and monoamine oxidase inhibitors used in the treatment of depression. Depression can occur if neurotransmitters in the brain do not function effectively.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antiderivative
DEFN: The antiderivative of a function ƒ ( x ) is a function F ( x ) whose derivative equals ƒ ( x ). The process of solving antiderivatives is called antidifferentiation or indefinite integration and its opposite operation is called differentiation , which is the process of finding a derivative. For example, the function F ( x ) = x 5 /5 is an antiderivative of f ( x ) = x 4 . Since the derivative of a constant is zero, x 4 has an infinite number of antiderivatives such as ( x 5 /5) - 10, ( x 5 /5) + 2, etc. Thus, all the antiderivatives of x 4 can be obtained by changing the value of C in F ( x ) = ( x 5 /5) + C ; where C is an arbitrary constant known as the constant of integration. Basically, the graphs of antiderivatives of a given function are vertical translations of each other, with each graph's location depending on the value of C . Antiderivatives are related to definite integrals through the fundamental theorem of calculus and they are important because they can be used to compute definite integrals.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antidifferentiation
DEFN: The reverse process of differentiation or a process of solving for antiderivatives. In other words, it is the process of finding an antiderivative for a given function; especially when deriving an indefinite integral or when evaluating a definite integral using the fundamental theorem of calculus rather than as an infinite sum of infinitesimal elements. The symbol f ( x )dx ∫ indicates the operation of antidifferentiation on f ( x ), which is the function being antidifferentiated or integrated and known as integrand. The antiderivative of a function f ( x ) is another function F( x ), whose derivative is f ( x )dx ∫ = F( x ) + C. The generalised form of the antiderivative of f ( x ) with respect to x is given as f ( x )dx ∫ = F( x ) + C, where C is called the constant of integration or arbitrary constant. This formula works because the derivative of a constant is zero. For example, x 2 is the antiderivative of 2 x , because 2 x is the derivative of x 2 .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antidiuretic hormone, adh
DEFN: A hormone produced in some mammals by specialised nerve cells in the hypothalamus, which is transported to, stored and secreted by the posterior pituitary gland. Its function is to regulate water re-absorption by the kidneys, thus reducing water loss in the urine. It also has various functions in the brain and is also released in response to a decrease in blood volume. An absence or low levels release of antidiuretic hormone causes diabetes insipidus.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antielectron
DEFN: The antiparticle or the antimatter counterpart of the electron. It has the same mass and the same properties as the electron, except for its electrical charge, which is positive (+1e) and a spin of ½. Like electrons, they appear as decay products of many heavier particles. When a low-energy antielectron collides with a low-energy electron, annihilation occurs, producing two 511 keV gamma rays travelling in opposite directions. Antielectrons can combine with other antiparticles to create atoms of antimatter.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antiemetic herbs
DEFN: Herbs that relieve or prevent nausea and vomiting. They include cloves, ginger, raspberry, lemongrass, lemons, and wild sage. Antiemetics work in the reverse way of emetics which are drugs or substances which induce nausea and vomiting A4 × 4 Anti Magic Square .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antifluorite structure
DEFN: A crystal structure for ionic compounds of the type M2 X, which is the same as the fluorite (CaF2 ) structure except that the positions of anions and cations are reversed, i.e. the cations occupy the F - sites and the anions occupy the Ca 2+ sites. Examples of the antifluorite structure are given by K2 O and K2 S.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antilogarithm
DEFN: The inverse function of the logarithm defined such that antilog b (log b n ) = antilog b z = b z = n . That is, if the log in base b of a number n , (i.e. log b n ), is z , then the antilogarithm in base b of z is therefore b z = n . For example, if the log in base 10 of 2, (i.e. log 10 2), is 0.3010, then the antilog in base 10 of 0.3010: (antilog10(0.3010) = antilog10(log102) = 100.3010 = 2. Also, if the log in base 2 of 8, (i.e. log2 8), is 3, then the antilog in base 2 of 3, i.e. antilog2 (log2 8) = antilog2 3 = 2 3 = 8. In general, if logarithm a b = c , then logarithm a c = b . If no base is specified, it is usually taken to be 10.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antimagic square
DEFN: A n n × nsquare arrangement of the numbers 1 to n 2 such that the totals of the n rows and n columns and two long diagonals form a sequence of (2 n + 2) consecutive integers. The smallest antimagic squares have order 4. An example of a 4 x 4 antimagic square is shown in the diagram. Note that the numbers in every row add up to the same number.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antineutrino
DEFN: A lepton, which is antiparticle of neutrinos. It is a neutral particle emitted in nuclear beta decay, where a neutron turns into a proton. It has a spin of ½. Antineutrinos, like neutrinos, interact with other matter only through the gravitational and weak forces, making them very difficult to detect experimentally.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antineutron
DEFN: The antimatter counter particle of the neutron, having the same mass, average lifetime, spin and magnitude of magnetic moment as the neutron. It has no net electric charge, but has opposite baryon number: +1 for neutron and^-1 for the antineutron. It was first produced in 1956 at the Bevatron particle accelerator at the University of California, Berkeley, by passing an antiproton beam through matter. Like any other antiparticle, it behaves like matter and can associate with other antiparticles, such as the positron and the antiproton, to form stable atoms. antinodes position displacement antinode Nodes and antinodes of a standing wave node t=0 t = τ t = 3τ λ λ λ t = τ t = τ node.

WORD: antipodal cell
DEFN: Any of the three haploid cells in the embryo sac of angiosperms located at the chalazal end opposite to the micropyle. They have no known function and degenerate after fertilisation. WORD: antisymmetric relation
DEFN: A binary relation * is said to be antisymmetric on a set Z if, for all x and y in Z , whenever x * y and y * x , then x = y .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antisymmetry principle
DEFN: The quantum mechanical principle that electrons must be described by wavefunctions which are antisymmetric with respect to interchange of the coordinates (including spin) of a pair of electrons. A corollary of the principle is the Pauli exclusion principle. All particles with half- integral spin (fermions) are described by antisymmetric wavefunctions and all particles with zero or integral spin (bosons) are described by symmetric wavefunctions.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: antlerite
DEFN: A green mineral consisting of basic copper sulfate with the chemical formula Cu 3 [OH] 4 SO4 and Mohs scale hardness of 3 to 3.5. It is a minor copper ore of widespread occurrence and formed by oxidation of primary copper minerals.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: apatite
DEFN: A common calcium phosphate mineral, with the chemical formula Ca 5 (PO4 ) 3 (OH,F,Cl), which has a hexagonal structure and occurs widely in igneous rocks, such as pegmatite and in metamorphic rocks, such as marbles. It is a source of phosphorus for plants and used in the manufacture of fertilisers. The enamel of teeth is made up mainly of this mineral.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aphrodisiacs
DEFN: Agents and substances that increases libido, or intensifies sexual desire as well as keep the body firm and tone up the tissues. They act on the mind and stimulate the arousal of the mood and are used to help restore normal sexual potency and function. They include herbs such as mahogany, cloves ginger, ginseng, nutmeg oil, white lotus and wild asparagus.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: apollonius' circle
DEFN: A circle, named after the Greek mathematician, Apollonius of Perga (261 BC^-190 BC) defined as the locus of a point C whose distance from a fixed point A is a multiple r of its distance from another fixed point B . If r is 1, then the locus is a line, the perpendicular bisector of the segment AB . If r is not equal to 1, then the locus is a circle.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: apollonius' theorem
DEFN: The equation that relates the length of a median in a triangle to the lengths of its sides. If a is the length of one side and b is the length of another, the third side is divided into two equal lengths c by a median of length m , then a 2 + b 2 = 2 m2 + 2 c 2 .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: apparent specific gravity
DEFN: The ratio of the mass of a volume of the impermeable portion of a material at a stated temperature to the mass of an equal volume of distilled water at a stated temperature.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: apparent viscosity
DEFN: The ratio of shear stress to shear rate in a fluid, calculated from measurements of forces and velocities as though the liquid were Newtonian (homogenous). It remains the same with changing shear strain rate at constant temperature and pressure. If the liquid is actually non-Newtonian, the apparent viscosity depends on the type and dimensions of the apparatus used.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: appearance temperature
DEFN: The temperature of the atomisation surface at which the analyte signal/noise (S/N) ratio reaches a value of 3 when the quantity of analyte in the atomiser is one hundred times the characteristic mass for peak high absorption.

WORD: appendix
DEFN: An outgrowth of the caecum or a small tube-shaped bag of tissue attached to the intestine. It is associated with the digestion of cellulose and is very large in herbivores, but in humans, it is a vestigial organ, serving no digestive function.

WORD: approximate integration
DEFN: Any of the various techniques for finding an approximate value for definite integral. There are many integrals that cannot be evaluated exactly and approximation techniques are used to estimate values for such integrals. WORD: approximation theory
DEFN: The branch of numerical analysis which is relative to finding a function f ( x ), which has approximately the same value as F ( x ) over a given interval.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aprotic
DEFN: A term used to describe a substance, especially a solvent that is non-protogenic or not capable of acting as a proton donor in a given situation. In other words, it is a polar solvent having a moderately high dielectric constant that does not contain replaceable hydrogen. With extremely strong Brønsted acids or bases, solvents that are normally aprotic may accept or lose a proton. For example, acetonitrile is in most instances an aprotic solvent, but it is protophilic in the presence of concentrated sulfuric acid and protogenic (capable of acting as a proton donor) in the presence of potassium tert -butoxide. Similar considerations apply to benzene, trichloromethane, etc.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: apse
DEFN: 1. Also known as apsidal point , it is any point at which the direction of motion of a point moving round a closed curve is perpendicular to its radius vector. Every point of a circle is thus an apse and the apsidal points of an ellipse are the endpoints of its axes. 2. Also known as apsides , it is a point on an orbit at which the motion of the orbiting object is at right angle to the central radius vector of the orbit. The apsidal distance (distance from an apse to the centre of the motion equals the radius vector at its maximum or minimum value.

WORD: aqua acid
DEFN: A type of acid in which the acidic hydrogen is on a water molecule coordinated to a metal ion. Examples are: Al(OH2 ) 6 3+ + H2 O → A1(OH2 ) 5 (OH) 2+ + H3 O + , Fe(H2 O) 6 3+ , Fe(H2 O) 6 2+ and Cr(H2 O) 6 3+ .

WORD: aqua regia
DEFN: A mixture of three parts of concentrated hydrochloric acid and one part of concentrated nitric acid by volume, which dissolves all metals except silver. Its ratio by volume is 3HCl:1HNO3 . It is used in the analysis of minerals and alloys and as a powerful cleaning agent.

WORD: aquaporin

WORD: aquation
DEFN: The incorporation of one or more integral molecules of water into another species with or without displacement of one or more other atoms or groups. For example, the incorporation of water into the inner ligand sphere of an inorganic complex is an aquation reaction.

WORD: aqueous
DEFN: 1. Also known as aqueous solution , it is a term used to refer to a solution in water or a solution where the solvent is water. It is often abbreviated to aq in a formula, for example, NaCl (aq) . There are three main types of reactions that occur in aqueous solutions: ion-exchange reactions, redox reactions and precipitation reaction (sometimes also known as ion-exchange reactions). Ion-exchange reactions occur between two substances, each consisting of positively and negatively charged ions that involve an exchange of one or more ionic components. The reaction can be represented by the following equation: AB(aq) + CD (aq) ⟶ AD + CB; either AD or CB may be a solid or a gas. In redox reactions, electrons are exchanged, with an ion losing electrons and becoming more positive, while the other ion gains electrons and becomes more negative, causing an increase or decrease in oxidation numbers. In a precipitation reaction, a precipitate is formed when ions in solution react with each other to form an insoluble product. 2. In geology, it means formed from matter deposited by water, as in certain sedimentary rocks.
------------------------------------------------------------

DEFN: The International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry (IUPAC) name for ammonium hydroxide, chemical formula NH3 (aq) .

WORD: archimedean spiral
DEFN: A curve whose equation in polar coordinates is r = aθ , where ( a > 0) is a constant. Equivalently, ( r , θ ) can be represented by the equation r = a + b / θ with real numbers a and b . It is the locus of points corresponding to the locations over time of a point moving away from a fixed point with a constant speed along a line which rotates with constant angular velocity. Changing the parameter a will turn the spiral, while b controls the distance between successive turnings. In the figure shown, the Archimedean spiral is described by the polar equation r = aθ , where a is a parameter determining the density of spiral turns. The separation distance between successive turnings in the Archimedian spiral is constant and equal to 2 πa . The following are the general formulas for converting the polar coordinates ( r , θ ) to Cartesian ones ( x , y ): x = r cos θ and y = r sin θ .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: archimedes
DEFN: A Greek mathematician who worked on levers and other aspects of mechanics. He formulated the Archimedes' principle and in hydrostatics, he devised a pump called the Archimedian Screw. His method of successive approximations allowed him to determine the value of π (pi), which is a constant in Euclidean plane geometry, defined as the ratio of a circle's circumference C to its diameter d: π = C /d, which equals to approximately 3.142. The ratio C /d is constant, regardless of a circle's size. For example, if a circle has twice the diameter d of another circle it will also have twice the circumference C , preserving the ratio C/d.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: archimedes' constant
DEFN: The ratio of a circle's circumference C to its diameter (d), expressed as: C/d, which equals to 22/7 or approximately 3.142.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: archimedes' method
DEFN: The method of estimating π by computing the area or perimeter of inscribed and circumscribed polygons for a unit circle using more and more sides. Archimedes estimated π to to be between 3 10 71 and 31 7 , that is 310 71 <π <3 1 7 . from computations with polygons with 96 sides. This gives 3.14 ...; being accurate to two decimal places.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: archimedes' number
DEFN: The dimensionless number, which is the ratio of gravitational force to viscous force. It characterises the motion of liquids due to density differences.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: archimedes' principle
DEFN: A law of physics named after its discoverer, Archimedes of Syracuse that concerns the upward force or upthrust on any object fully or partly submerged in a fluid. It states that the buoyant force on an object is equal to the weight of the fluid it displaces. Mathematically, (buoyant force) Fb = m f g ... ., where m f is the mass of the fluid displaced by the body. Archimedes' principle can be expressed in other words that an object in a fluid such as water experiences an upthrust from the fluid which is equal to the weight of the fluid that the body displaces. The upward force is called the upthrust or buoyant force, expressed mathematically as: Upthrust = weight in air - weight in liquid. The resultant force on the A body is the difference between the upthrust and the body's weight. This difference is zero for a floating body, ie. upthrust and weight are equal and opposite. Hot air displaces a balloon, producing upthrust. This upthrust is balanced by the weight of the balloon. A hot air balloon rises when the upthrust on it is greater than its weight. A diver may attach lead weight to his suit if he wants to reach a greater depth. His weight will then be greater than the upthrust on him. For a given body to float in a given fluid depends on its weight and volume or the relative density (weight per unit volume). This is compared to that of the fluid in order to determine its buoyant force. If the body is less dense than the fluid, it will float. If the body is denser than the fluid, it will sink. The principle applies to both floating and submerged bodies and to all fluids, i.e., liquids and gases. Archimedes' principle is an important and underlying concept in the field of fluid mechanics.

DEFN: The area of a surface obtained as a result of rotating, through one revolution about the x -axis, the arc of the curve y = f ( x ) between x = a and x = b is given by 2 π y 1 + dy dx ⎛ ⎝ ⎜⎜⎜ ⎞ ⎠ ⎟⎟⎟ a b ∫ dx , where y = f ( x ) is a graph of the function f ( x ) such that f '( x ) is continuous on the interval [ a , b ] and f ( x ) ≥ 0 for all x in [ a , b ].

WORD: argand diagram
DEFN: Named after Jean-Robert Argand (1768-1822), Swiss-born mathematician, it is a two-dimensional method for representing complex numbers by Cartesian coordinates x , y . Along the x -axis or horizontal axis are plotted the real numbers and along the y -axis or the vertical axis the non-real or imaginary numbers. The number x + iy is represented by the point ( x , y ) or by the corresponding vector. If the polar coordinates of ( x , y ) are ( r , θ ), r is the modulus and θ the argument of x + iy .

WORD: armstrong number
DEFN: An n -digit number, which is equal to the sum of its digits raised to the n th power. WORD: aromatic

They undergo substitution reactions more readily than addition reactions. 2. Having an aroma such as onions, leeks, garlic and scallions; fragrant or sweet-smelling.

WORD: aromatic hydrocarbons
DEFN: A member of the class of hydrocarbons, of which benzene is the first member, consisting of assemblages of cyclic conjugated carbon atoms and characterised by large resonance energies. In other words, they are a class of hydrocarbons having a closed ring system of carbon atoms with delocalised π- electrons. They resist addition reactions and common oxidising agents. They can be monocyclic (MAH) or polycyclic (PAH).
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aromaticity
DEFN: A state in which three (3) or more carbon atoms present in an unsaturated organic compound joined in a ring that exhibit delocalisation of π -electrons as in benzene and its compounds. In other words, it is a term used to describe the cyclic delocalisation of a π -electron system of a conjugated compound. A molecule or ion with such delocalised electrons is represented by several contributing structures called resonance structures or canonical forms. When a molecule cannot be completely represented by a single structure but its chemical and physical properties can be described by two or more different structures, then the true structure is said to be resonance hybrid, which is the overall combination of all canonical forms. It is more stable than all canonical forms and shows all the characteristics of the molecule. This phenomenon is called resonance effect. All C-C bonds are equivalent and the ring is flat or almost flat. The total number of delocalised π -electrons must obey the Hὕkel Rule, 4 n + 2, where n takes the values 0, 1, 2, 3,...

WORD: arrangement
DEFN: 1. A permutation (in ordered sequence) or combination (not in ordered sequence) of a set of objects. This is given by n p r = n ! ( n - r )! . 2. In algebra, any ordered sequence of elements. For example, 3,1,2 is an arrangement, whereas the permutation 2,6,8 → 8,6,2 is an operation upon arrangements.

WORD: array
DEFN: 1. An orderly arrangement of numbers or objects, often in rows, columns or a matrix that is most commonly used as a visual tool for demonstrating multiplication and division. 2. An n -dimensional ordered set of data items identified by a single name and one or more indices, so that each element of the set is individually addressable. Examples are matrices and vectors. 3. In computer science, it is a group of related data values grouped together as one value. It is used in computer programs to organise data so that a related set of values can be easily sorted or searched.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: arrhenius equation
DEFN: A mathematical relation, named after Svante Arrhenius (1859-1927), which expresses the exponential relationship between the rate constant and the activation energy of a reaction at a particular temperature. It is expressed as k = A exp( - E a / RT ), where k is the rate constant of a given reaction and Ea the activation energy. A is a constant for a given reaction, called the pre-exponential factor. Often the equation is written in logarithmic form ln k = ln A - E a / RT . A graph of ln k against 1/ T is a straight line with a gradient - E a / R and an intercept on the ln k axis of ln A .

WORD: arsenic
DEFN: A brittle, greyish-white metalloid or semi-metallic element, with the chemical symbol As, atomic number 33, atomic weight 74.92160, melting point 817 °C (1 090.15 K) at 28 atmospheres pressure, sublimation point 613 °C (886.15 K). It consists of very poisonous compounds, especially the oxide (As 2 O3 ). It was discovered in 1250 and is a member of Group 15 (VA) of the Periodic Table, it has three allotropic forms: yellow, black and grey and found widely distributed in nature. However, the main sources are realgar and arsenopyrite. It is used in the manufacture of semi-conductors in electronics, glass manufacturing to remove green colour caused by impurities of iron compounds, hardening of lead and in the manufacture of military poison gases. Arsenic also means relating to or containing arsenic, especially with a valence of 5.

It occurs naturally as arsenolite.

WORD: arsenic

WORD: ascoli's theorem
DEFN: The result that a family of functions that is point-wise bounded and on a compact space is totally bounded in the supremum norm. Every sequence in such a family will contain a norm convergent subsequence. In the complex case, this is known as the Arzela-Ascoli theorem . It is named after the Italian analyst Giulio Ascoli (1843-96).

WORD: ascorbic acid
DEFN: A white or light yellow solid organic compound with antioxidant properties found in fresh fruits and vegetables. Its chemical formula is C6 H8 O6 , melting point 190-192 °C, 463-465 K and density of 1.65 g/cm3 . It is prepared industrially from glucose in a method based on the Reichstein process. It acts as a cofactor in the hydroxylation of proline to hydroxy-proline. Overcooking of vegetables reduces their vitamin C content. Lack of it causes skin sores or ulcers, mouth and gum problems, scurvy, etc.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: asparagine
DEFN: An uncharged polar amino acid with the chemical formula C4 H8 N2 O3 . It is found in many proteins and present in large amounts in some plants, such as asparagus, that is easily hydrolysed to aspartic acid. Because ammonia is extremely toxic to living cells, it is used to synthesise glutamine and asparagine, in which form it can be stored for later use in amino acid synthesis.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aspartame
DEFN: A non-carbohydrate sweetener used in food under various trade names such as Naturasweet, Canderel and Equal. Aspartame consists of two amino acids, aspartic acid forming about 40% by weight and phenylalanine forming about 50% combined with a methyl ester. Its molecular formula is C14 H18 N2 O5 and molecular weight is 294.31. It is about 200 times as sweet as sugar and unlike saccharine, has no after taste. Aspartame loses its sweetness when heated and it is usually not suitable for baking. Aspartame is favoured as a sweetener by those on low carbohydrate diet such as diabetics, some athletes and actors/actresses and all other people who need to watch their weights. Drinks such as Diet Coca Cola, Coca Cola Zero, Diet Pepsi, some chewing gums such as Wrigleys Airwaves and Orbit and some other store-bought foods such as cereals contain aspartame. Some other artificial non-caloric sweeteners include saccharin, sucralose, neotame and acesulfame potassium. Some other natural product sugar substitutes include sorbitol or xylitol.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aspartic acid
DEFN: An acidic amino acid with the formula C4 H7 NO4 , density 1.7 g/cm3 , melting point 270 °C (543 K) and boiling point of 324 °C, 597 K (decomposes). It is formed by a transamination, in which the amino group of glutamic acid is transferred to oxaloacetic acid so forming aspartic acid. Breakdown is by the reverse reaction, followed by further oxidation in the TCA cycle. Aspartate is an important precursor of nitrogenous compounds. The amino acids leucine, isoleucine, threonine, methionine and lysine are all derived from aspartic acid and it is necessary for synthesis of purines, pyrimidines and porphyrins. Aspartate also acts as an amino- group donor in several reactions.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aspect experiment
DEFN: A two-photon correlation experiment conducted by French physicist, Alain Aspect and his colleagues in 1982 to investigate the validity of the entanglement effect in quantum mechanics by using some kind of Bell inequality. Bell's Theorem states that a Bell inequality must be obeyed under any local hidden variable theory but can in certain circumstances be violated under quantum mechanics. The experiment involves producing photons from a source of excited calcium ions. A Bell test experiment is one designed to test whether or not the real world obeys a Bell inequality.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aspect ratio
DEFN: An image projection attribute that describes the proportional relationship between the width of an object and its height. It is commonly expressed as two numbers separated by a colon, as in 16:9. For example, movies which are usually short with a wide angle lens have an aspect ratio that is typically 16.9, which means that the width of the image area is almost twice its height .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: aspirin
DEFN: A naturally occurring carboxylic acid, with the chemical formula CH3 COOC6 H4 COOH, density 1.40 g/cm³, melting point 135°C (408 K) and boiling point 140 °C (413 K) and decoposes. Aspirin is part of a group of medications called nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs). It is commonly used in tablet form to relieve pain and reduce fever and inflammation and also as an antiplatelet agent by inhibiting the production of thromboxane. It is also used long-term, at low doses, to help prevent heart attacks, strokes and thrombosis or blood clot formation in people at high risk of developing blood clots. Low doses of aspirin may be given immediately after a heart attack to reduce the risk of another heart attack or of the death of cardiac tissue. The undesirable side-effects of aspirin taken by mouth include gastrointestinal ulcers, stomach bleeding and tinnitus, especially in higher doses.

WORD: assignment
DEFN: 1. In logic, a function that associates specific elements of domain with each free variable in a formal calculus. 2. In computing, a statement in a program that allocates a value to a variable usually written as in x := y + z . If the new value is a function of the previous value of that same variable the notation is still used; thus x := x + 1 is an instruction to increase the value of x by one.

WORD: associate numbers

WORD: association
DEFN: 1. An ecological phenomenon in which two or more species occur in closer proximity to one another than would be expected on the basis of chance. 2. In chemistry, it is the combination of molecules of one substance with those of another to form chemical species that are held together by forces weaker than normal chemical bonds. 3. In mathematics, it is also known as correlation , which is the degree of relationship between two sets of information. If one set of data increases at the same time as the other, the relationship is said to be positive or direct and if one set of data increases as the other decreases the relationship is said to be negative or inverse. Mathematically, a correlation is expressed by a correlation coefficient that ranges from^-1 (never occur together), through 0 (absolutely independent), to 1 (always occur together).

WORD: associative
DEFN: 1. Of a binary operation, having the property that the bracketing of its arguments may be disregarded since ( a • b ) • c = a • ( b • c ), where • is the operator. 2. Of an algebraic structure, possessing an associative operator. For example, in a + ( b + c ) = ( a + b ) + c , the operation indicated by + is associative.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: associative law
DEFN: One of the laws of algebra, it is a mathematical law stating that the value of an expression is independent of the grouping of the symbols or terms in the expression under operation. For example, the associative law of addition states that numbers may be added in any order, for example, ( x + y ) + z = x +( y + z ), ( a + b ) + c = a + ( b + c ) = ( a + c ) + b and for multiplication ( xy ) z = x ( yz ), x , y , z ∊ R. But for subtraction and division, the law does not apply. For example, (3 + 5) + 2 = 3 + (5 + 2) = (3 + 2) + 5 = 10 and (3 × 5) × 2 = 3 × (5 × 2) = (3 × 2) × 5 = 30 but (7 - 3) - 5 ≠ 7 - (3 - 5) ⇒ -1 ≠ 9, 2 ≠ 8,.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: associative operation
DEFN: An operation in mathematics in which the outcome is independent on the grouping of the numbers or symbols concerned. If * is a binary operation defined on the set of real numbers R , then the operation * is associative if ( x * y ) * z = x * ( y * z ).
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: astatine
DEFN: A non-metallic unstable radioactive element with the symbol At, atomic number 85, melting point 302 °C (575.15 K) and boiling point 337 °C (610.15 K). It was discovered in 1940. Its most stable and longest lived isotope has a mass number of 210 and a half-life of 8.3 hours. It is the heaviest in the halogen group. Very rare in nature, it is obtained by bombardment of bismuth with alpha particles. It is used in medicine as a tracer element and in cancer treatment. Like iodine, it is known to accumulate in the thyroid gland.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: astronomical telescope
DEFN: A balloon-borne telescope designed for gathering light and viewing astronomical objects. It consists of two converging lenses, as the objective lens and the eyepiece lens. The objective lens has long focal length whereas the eyepiece lens has short focal length. This ensures that the telescope has a high magnifying power. The objective lens collects parallel rays from a distant object and forms a real inverted and diminished image L1 at the principal focus F0 of the lens. The eyepiece then acts as a magnifying lens and forms a magnified virtual image of L1 at L2 . When the principal focus of the objective lens is arranged to coincide with that of the eyepiece lens, the final image is formed at infinity. The telescope is used to view distant objects such as stars and planets. WORD: astronomical unit
DEFN: A unit of distance used in astronomy and defined by the International Astronomical Union as the length for which the Gaussian gravitational constant ( k ) takes the value 0.017,202,098,95, when the units of measurement are the astronomical units of length, mass and time. It is equal to about 149,597,870.7 kilometres, which is the mean distance between the Sun and the Earth. Light travels this distance in approximately 8.3 minutes. It is a derived constant and used to indicate distances within the solar system.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: asymptote
DEFN: A line or a curve that approaches a given curve arbitrarily closely. In other words, it is a straight line whose perpendicular distance from a curve decreases to zero as the distance from the origin increases without limit. For example, a curve in the form y = R( x ), where R( x ) is a rational function of x and its asymptotes are at the point where the denominator disappears or where the function ceases to exist. A function y = 1/( x^-1) does not exist at x = 1, therefore, the line x = 1 is an asymptote to the curve y = 1/( x^-1). A line, x = a to a curve is a vertical asymptote, the line y = b to a curve is a horizontal asymptote and the line y = mx + b to a curve is an oblique asymptote. The graph of y = 1/( x^-1) with the asymptote x = 1 is a tangent to the curve at infinity. Asymptote Vertical asymptote Horizontal asymptote y x Scores Frequency Normal Curve.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: asymptotic
DEFN: 1. Of a function, or series, approaching a given value, the asymptote, closely, as the independent variable or some expression containing a variable, approaches a limit or tends to infinity. Graphically , t h e perpendicular distance between a curve and its asymptote tends to zero as the distance from the origin tends to a limit or infinity. For example, a normal curve, also known as normal distribution curve or Gaussian curve is asymptotic as shown as shown in the diagram. 2. Of a formula, becoming increasingly exact as a variable approaches a limit, usually infinity. 3. Of a pair of functions, approaching each other infinitely closely as their arguments tend to a certain value, normally infinity. This is usually expressed as f ( x ) is asymptotic to h ( x ) at u if lim x → u f ( x ) h ( x ) = 1.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: asymptotic function
DEFN: Two functions are said to be asymptotic if they approach each other infinitely closely as their argument tend to a certain value, normally infinity. This is usually expressed as f ( x ) is asymptotic to h ( x ) at u if lim x → u ( f ( x )/ h ( x ) = 1.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: asymptotic series
DEFN: A series formed by the expansion of a function in a certain form, such that the error resulting from terminating the series at a certain term tends to zero more rapidly than a certain other, as the independent variable x tends to infinity. An asymptotic series expansion is not necessarily a convergent series. For example, a series of the form a 0 + a 1 x + a 2 x 2 + ... + anxn + ... is an asymptotic series of the function f ( x ) if there exists a number N such that for all n ≠ N the quantity x n [ f ( x ) - S n ( x )] approaches zero as x approaches infinity, where S n ( x ) is the sum of the first n terms in the series.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: atom
DEFN: 1. The smallest particle of an element that can exist and has the element's properties or the smallest unit of matter that can take part in a chemical reaction and that cannot be broken down chemically into anything simpler. It is a basic unit of matter that is made up of a dense central nucleus consisting of small dense positively charged protons and neutrons surrounded by negatively charged electrons. The nucleus of hydrogen^-1 is the only stable nuclide with no neutrons. The electrons of an atom are bound to the nucleus by the electromagnetic force and a group of atoms can remain bound to each other to form a molecule. An atom containing an equal number of protons and electrons is electrically neutral, otherwise it has a positive charge if there are fewer electrons (electron deficiency) or negative charge if there are more electrons (electron excess). An atom whether positively or negatively charged is known as an ion . An atom is classified according to the number of protons and neutrons in its nucleus, with the number of protons determining the chemical element and the number of neutrons determining the isotope of the element. 2. A set, often a point, in a measure space with strictly positive measure and such that any subset of the set either has equal or zero measure. 3. A minimal non-zero element in a Boolean algebra.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: atomic
DEFN: 1. Extremely small; minute. 2. Of, using or characterised by the energy that is produced when atoms are split apart, for example, atomic bomb. 3. Of a substance, consisting of uncombined atoms rather than molecules. 4. In logic, of a sentence, formula, etc., having no internal structure at the appropriate level of analysis. In predicate calculus, Fa is an atomic sentence and Fx an atomic predicate. 5. Also called purely atomic , a measurable set which has positive measure and contains no set of smaller but positive measure.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: atomic charge
DEFN: The electric charge of an ion, equal to the number of electrons the atom has gained or lost in its ionisation multiplied by the charge on one electron. WORD: atomic force microscopy
DEFN: A type of microscopy in which a small probe, consisting of a tiny chip diamond, is held on a spring-loaded cantilever in contact with the surface of the sample. The probe is moved slowly across the surface and the tracking force between the tip and the surface is monitored. The probe is raised and lowered so as to keep this force constant and a profile of the surface is produced. Scanning the probe over the sample gives a computer- generated contour map of the surface.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: atomic mass constant
DEFN: One twelfth (1/12th) of the mass of a carbon^-12 atom in its nuclear and electronic ground state, m u = 1.6605402 (10) x 10 -27 kg. It is equal to the unified atomic mass unit.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: atomic mass unit
DEFN: A unit used to express the mass of individual atoms. It is expressed as the mass equal to 1/12 th of the mass of a carbon^-12 isotope. It has a value of 1.6605402 (10) × 10 -27 kg. A mass expressed on this standard is called a relative atomic mass (r.a.m.), with the symbol A r . The symbol for amu is u and that for dalton is Da. One dalton is approximately equal to the mass of one proton or one neutron.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: atomic orbital
DEFN: 1. A mathematical function that describes the wave-like behaviour of either one electron or a pair of electrons in an atom. This function can be used to calculate the probability of finding any electron of an atom in any specific region around the atom's nucleus. The term may thus refer directly to the physical region defined by the function where the electron is likely to be; or a region in space around the nucleus of an atom, where there is a high probability of finding a specified electron. Orbitals of the same principal quantum number belong to the same subshell. 2. In physics, it is the region in which an electron may be found around a single atom.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: atomic radiation
DEFN: Energy given out by disintegrating atoms during radioactive decay. The energy may be in the form of fast moving particles known as alpha particles and beta particles or in the waveform of high energy electromagnetic waves known as gamma radiation. Prolonged exposure to atomic radiation can lead to radiation sicknesses.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: atomic weight
DEFN: Former name for relative atomic mass , defined as weighted average of the masses of the constituent isotopes of a chemical element or the ratio of the average mass of a chemical element's atoms to 1/12 th the mass of an atom of the carbon^-12 isotope. Its symbol is Ar. Measuring the masses of atoms in grams, for example, would imply dealing with extremely small numbers, thus, the mass of an atom is compared with that of an atom of carbon^-12. In calculating the relative atomic mass of an element with isotopes, the relative mass and proportion of each is taken into account. For example, naturally occurring chlorine consists of atoms of relative isotopic masses 35 (75%) and 37 (25%). Its relative atomic mass is 35.5. That is A r = 100 × 35 ⎛ ⎝ ⎜⎜⎜⎜ ⎞ ⎠ ⎟⎟⎟⎟+ 25 100 × 37 ⎛ ⎝ ⎜⎜⎜⎜ ⎞ ⎠ ⎟⎟⎟⎟= 35 . 5 . The relative masses of atoms are measured using an instrument called A a mass spectrometer, invented by the English physicist Francis William Aston (1877-1945). The cations formed are brought down on to a detector in turn according to their mass-to-charge ratio. The instrument provides a measure of the relative mass (compared to 12 C) and the relative number of each isotope. Through the use of this instrument he discovered the existence of isotopes of elements. Relative atomic masses have no units.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: atomicity
DEFN: The number of atoms in a molecule of an element. It is denoted by the letter Z. For example, oxygen (O2 ) has an atomicity of 2, ozone (O3 ) an atomicity of 3 and benzene (C6 H6 ) an atomicity of 12.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: atpase
DEFN: A family of enzymes that brings about the hydrolysis of adenosine triphosphate (ATP) into adenosine diphosphate (ADP) with a release of energy. There are different forms of membrane-associated ATPases to meet specific demands of cells. They include the following: (i) The F-Adenosine triphosphate ATPases (phosphorylation factor, also known as H+-transporting Adenosine triphosphate ATPases or F(0)F(1)-Adenosine triphosphate ATPases, V-Adenosine triphosphate ATPases or Vacuole Adenosine triphosphate (ATP)ases, A-Adenosine triphosphate ATPases or Archaea Adenosine triphosphate ATPases, P-Adenosine triphosphate ATPases also known as E1-E2 Adenosine triphosphate ATPases and E-Adenosine triphosphate ATPases or Extracellular Adenosine triphosphate ATPases.

WORD: atropine
DEFN: A poisonous crystalline alkaloid, with the chemical formula C17 H23 NO3 . It is isolated from deadly nightshade ( Atropa belladonna) . It inhibits muscarinic acetylcholine receptors and also causes dilation of the pupil and hence used extensively in eye surgery.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: attenuance filter
DEFN: An optical Attenuate Leaf Base filter, which reduces the radiant power of a light beam by a constant factor over all wavelengths within its operating range.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: attenuation coefficient
DEFN: Ratio of the natural log of 2 to the half-value thickness of material. It is a constant used to calculate the intensity of X-rays given any thickness of material. It shows how easily a material can be penetrated by a beam of light, sound, particles, or other energy or matter. Its symbol is μ. A large attenuation coefficient means that the beam is quickly weakened as it passes through the medium, and a small attenuation coefficient means that the medium is relatively transparent to the beam. The SI unit of attenuation coefficient is the reciprocal metre (m^-1 ).
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: attractor
DEFN: 1. A set of physical properties toward which a system tends to evolve, regardless of the starting conditions of the system. 2. In mathematics, in a dynamical system, a value or set of values toward which variables tend to evolve for a wide variety of starting conditions of the system. It is defined formally as a closed set E such that for a given mapping f , f (E) is contained in E and for all x in some E, the distance from f m ( x ) to E tends to zero as m tends to infinity. It is usually required that the orbit of f is dense in E for some value of x . If the attractor, E, is a fractal set, it is said to be strange . For example, in the Julia sets, z → z 2 - μ makes E strange for some values of μ.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: auger electron spectroscopy
DEFN: Any technique in which a specimen is bombarded with keV-energy electrons or X-rays and the energy distribution of the electrons produced through radiationless de-excitation of the atoms in the sample (Auger electrons) is recorded. The derivative curve may also be recorded.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: augmentation
DEFN: The enlargement of a set of equations or a matrix in linear programming, matrix theory or control theory.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: augmented lagrangian
DEFN: Any of a number of combinations of a penalty function term, usually quadratic, with a Lagrangian, in order to produce algorithms that exploit the best features of both.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: augmented matrix
DEFN: The coefficient matrix that has an extra column containing the constant terms, this extra column being separated by a vertical line. It is created by joining the coefficient of the matrix to the numbers on the right hand side of the equations. The new column is set apart by a dotted vertical line. For example, for a system of linear equations 2 x^-3 y = 8 and 4 x + 5 y = 1, the augmented matrix is given as: . The solution of the augmented matrix can be obtained by transforming it to reduced echelon form by elementary row reduction operations.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: autocorrelation
DEFN: A measure of the correlation of a particular time series delayed by k lags (the distance between the observations that are so correlated). It is obtained by dividing the covariance between two observations, separated by k lags, of a time series (auto-covariance) by the standard deviation y t and y k^-1 . If the autocorrelation is calculated for all values of k the autocorrelation function is obtained. For a time series that does not change over time, the autocorrelation function decreases exponentially to zero.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: automorphic function
DEFN: An analytic function, f , on a domain D, such that for a group of Mobius transformations, T, T( z ) is in D and f (T( z )) = f ( z ) for all z in D.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: automorphic number
DEFN: A number n , whose square ends with n . For example, 5 is automorphic beacuse 5 2 = 25, which ends with 5. A number n is called trimorphic , if n 3 ends in n . An example is 49, since 49 3 = 117649. Not all trimorphic numbers are automorphic. A number n is called tri-automorphic if 3 n 2 ends in n . An example is 667 because 3 x 667 2 is 1334667, ends with 667.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: autonomous
DEFN: 1. A differential equation or a system of ordinary differential equations is said to be autonomous if it does not explicitly have an independent variable in the equation dy dt = f ( y ). For example, the equation dx dt = x is autonomous, but dx dt = t is not. 2. Of an ordinary differential equation of the second order, able to be reduced to a first-order equation by writing du dt = p, d 2 u dt 2 = p dp du , and regarding u as the independent variable, where u is the dependent variable and t the independent variable of the given equation.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: autoprotolysis
DEFN: A proton (hydron) transfer reaction between two identical molecules, usually a solvent, one acting as a Brønsted acid and the other as a Brønsted base. For example, two water molecules (H2 O + H2 O) self-ionises to hydroxide ion (OH - ) and hydronium ion (H3 O + ) as follows: H2 O + H2 O ⇌ OH - + H3 O + .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: autoregressive model
DEFN: A model that is used in time series analysis. In the model x t observation, at time t is a linear function of the previous values of the series. The first order of autoregressive model is given by x t = φ x t^-1 + α t , where α t is a random disturbance and φ is a parameter of the model. The corresponding model of order p is given by: x t = φ 1 x t^-1 + φ 2 x t^-2 + ... + φ p x t - p + α t .
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: autoregressive moving-average model
DEFN: The combination of autoregressive model and a moving-average model in time series. The general model of order p , q which is denoted by ARMA( p , q ) is X t = φ 1 x t^-1 + φ 2 x t^-2 + ... + φ p x t-p + α t - θ 1 α t^-1 - ... - θ q α t - q , where φ 1 , φ 2 K, φ q and θ 1 , θ 2 K, θ q are the parameters of the model and α t , α t^-1 K, α t-q ... are white noise sequences.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: autoregulation
DEFN: The process by which a gene regulates its own expression, especially the maintenance by an organ or tissue of a constant blood flow despite changes in arterial pressure; and the adjustment of blood flow through an organ in accordance with its metabolic needs.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: autosum
DEFN: A function used in Microsoft Excel spreadsheet program to find the sum of various numbers, which inserts a formula in the selected cell that adds the numbers in the column above it.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: autotransformer
DEFN: A transformer that has a single winding, Autotransformer input voltage output voltage output voltage which is tapped at different points to provide both the primary and secondary circuits. It has many uses and applications including to start induction motors, to regulate the voltage of transmission lines and to transform voltages when the primary to secondary ratio is close to unity. Its advantages include being smaller, lighter and cheaper than typical dual-winding transformers. Some of its disadvantages include the following: (i) it does not provide electrical isolation, so that a failure of the insulation of the windings can result in full input voltage being applied to the output. (ii) It cannot safely be used for stepping down higher voltages to much lower voltages suitable for smaller loads. (iii) A break in the part of the winding that is used as both primary and secondary will result in the transformer acting as an inductor in series with the load, which under light load conditions may result in near full input voltage being applied to the output. (iv) If the secondary circuit suffers a short-circuit condition, the resulting primary current would be much larger than an equivalent double wound transformer due to the increased flux linkage damaging the autotransformer.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: autotroph
DEFN: Any living organism that synthesises organic substances from inorganic molecules by using light or chemical energy. Autotrophic nutrition or holophytic nutrition is characteristic of organisms possessing chlorophyll (eg. Cynobacteria, Euglena, Spirogyra/green algae, green plants) by trapping light and combining inorganic substances, carbon dioxide and water to produce food by photosynthesis. Autotrophs are the primary producers in all food chains since the materials they synthesise are the energy sources of all other organisms. Autotrophs produce their own sugars, lipids and amino acids using carbon dioxide as a source of carbon and ammonia or nitrates as a source of nitrogen. Organisms such as the green plants, certain algae and the pigmented sulfur bacteria that use light energy to synthesise organic compounds are called photosynthetic autotrophs . Organisms including the iron bacteria, the nitrifying bacteria and the nonpigmented sulfur bacteria that oxidise such compounds as hydrogen sulfide (H2 S) to obtain energy are called chemosynthetic autotrophs or chemotrophs . Heterotrophs are organisms that must obtain their energy from organic compounds. Autotrophic means capable of synthesising organic substances from inorganic molecules by using light or chemical energy.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: auxiliary equation
DEFN: Any simpler equation that assists the solution of a given more difficult one, usually obtained by taking transforms; especially an equation of the same form as a given A differential equation but with seal variables replacing the derivatives. For example, the auxiliary equation d 2 x dy 2 + b dx dy + cy = 0 is D2 + bD + c = 0.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: auxin
DEFN: A class of plant growth hormones that promote stem and root growth and development. They influence many aspects of plant growth and development, including cell enlargement, inhibition of development of axillary buds, tropisms and the initiation of roots. Some of the most important auxins include: indole^-3-acetic acid, 4-chloroindole^-3- acetic acid, Indole^-3-butyric acid and 2-phenylacetic acid.
------------------------------------------------------------
WORD: available nutrient
DEFN: Any element or compound in the soil that is present in the correct chemical form and ratio that can be readily absorbed and assimilated by growing plants. Available nutrients may be derived from the soil or the atmosphere.

WORD: average deviation
DEFN: It is the arithmetic mean of the absolute deviations. Its symbol is μ . Statistically, it is defined as μ = X i - X i = 1 N ∑ N = X1 - X + X2 - X + ... + X N - X N , where X1 , X2 , ..., X N are the sample points and X is the sample mean based on X1 , X2 , ..., X N .
